Kähler metrics and toric Lagrangian fibrations Thanks: RLF was partially supported by NSF grants DMS-2003223 and DMS-2303586. MM was supported by the Max Planck Institute for Mathematics.
Rui Loja Fernandes, Maarten Mol

Abstract
. We extend the Abreu-Guillemin theory of invariant Kähler metrics from toric symplectic manifolds to any symplectic manifold admitting a toric action of a symplectic torus bundle. We show that these are precisely the symplectic manifolds admitting a Lagrangian fibration with elliptic singularities. The base of such a toric Lagrangian fibration is a codimension 0 submanifold with corners of an integral affine manifold, called a Delzant subspace. This concept generalizes the Delzant polytope associated with a compact symplectic toric manifold. Given a Delzant subspace of finite type, we provide a Delzant-type construction of a Lagrangian fibration with the moment image being the specified Delzant subspace. We establish a 1:1 correspondence between invariant Kähler metrics and a pair consisting of an elliptic connection on the total space of the fibration and a hybrid b b -metric on the base Delzant subspace, both with specified residues over the facets. Finally, we characterize extremal invariant Kähler metrics as those whose scalar curvature descends to an affine function on the base integral affine manifold. We show that this provides a method for finding and constructing extremal Kähler metrics.



1.
Introduction

In the late 1990s, Guillemin
[
24
]
and Abreu
[
1
]
described all invariant, compatible Kähler metrics for a compact symplectic toric manifold, in terms of singular Hessian metrics in the associated Delzant polytope. Abreu’s work also encompasses a fourth-order nonlinear PDE expressing the condition for an invariant Kähler metric to be extremal in the sense of Calabi. Subsequently, Donaldson
[
16
]
developed the analysis of Abreu’s equation and formulated K-stability for polytopes, sparking a series of subsequent research works in the search for extremal Kähler metrics (see, e.g., the recent survey
[
27
]
). The main aim of this work is to extend the Abreu-Guillemin theory to a wider class of symplectic manifolds.

The class of symplectic manifolds we consider here have a type of symmetry which is described by an action of a symplectic torus bundle
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
. This type of action includes as a special case the ordinary Hamiltonian torus actions but also, e.g., torus actions with cylinder valued moment maps or even examples with no globally defined Hamiltonian torus action. More precisely, we consider
toric
actions of symplectic torus bundles
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
(see Definition
4.1
). These can be characterized in terms of their moment maps as
toric Lagrangian fibrations
, i.e., singular Lagrangian fibrations admitting only elliptic-type singularities (see Theorem
4.4
):

Theorem
.

Every toric Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space is a toric Lagrangian fibration. Conversely, every toric Lagrangian fibration admits a toric Hamiltonian action of a symplectic torus bundle
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
.

We picture a toric Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space as in the following diagram

\textstyle{(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces(S,\omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\circlearrowright

\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{M}

where
M
is an integral affine manifold,
p:(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
is the corresponding symplectic torus bundle,
(S,\omega)
is a symplectic manifold and
\mathcal{T}
acts on
S
along the moment map
\mu
.

Delzant’s classification of toric symplectic manifolds
[
15
]
has been extended to a classification of toric
\mathcal{T}
-spaces in terms of their moment map image
\Delta=\mu(S)

[
28
]
(see also Section
4
). The space
\Delta
is now a codimension 0 submanifold with corners of the integral affine manifold
M
, called a
Delzant subspace
. A particular case of these are the principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces, which correspond to Lagrangian fibrations without singularities, where the classification reduces to Duistermaat’s result in
[
19
]
(see also Section
3
). The classical symplectic toric manifolds, both compact and non-compact (see
[
26
]
), also fit into this framework and can be characterized as the toric
\mathcal{T}
-spaces whose associated Delzant space has trivial affine holonomy (see Theorem
4.16
). This last result is one instance of the fact that the integral affine geometry of
M
plays a crucial role in the theory of toric
\mathcal{T}
-spaces. This is also a well-known feature of the theory of integrable systems (see, e.g.,
[
20
,
32
]
).

Given an integral affine manifold
M
and a Delzant subspace
\Delta\subset M
, the classification mentioned above requires the construction of a toric
\mathcal{T}
-space

\mu:(S,\omega)\to M

with
\mu(S)=\Delta
. This is done for general Delzant subspaces in
[
28
]
. Here, for a large class of Delzant subspaces, we give another construction in the spirit of Delzant’s original construction. This is the content of the following theorem (see Section
5
for details).

Theorem
.

Let
\Delta\subset M
be a finite type Delzant subspace with
d
facets. Every toric
\mathcal{T}
-space with moment map image
\Delta
can be realized as a symplectic quotient

\big((P\times\mathbb{C}^{d})\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}(\Gamma\ltimes\mathbb{T}^{d}),\,\omega_{\mathrm{red}}\big),

(1.1)

with
P
a principal Hamiltonian
p^{*}\mathcal{T}
-space, where
p:\tilde{M}\to M
is the universal covering space. When the Lagrangian-Chern class vanishes one can take
P=p^{*}\mathcal{T}
.

As we explain in Section
2
, in spite of the fact that an element of a symplectic torus bundle only acts on a single fiber of the moment map, one can still make sense of
\mathcal{T}
-invariant objects. In particular, one can consider
\mathcal{T}
-invariant metrics and complex structures. Using this we extend the Abreu-Guillemin theory to toric
\mathcal{T}
-spaces. Our main result is the following (see Section
7
).

Theorem
.

Let
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
be a toric
\mathcal{T}
-space, with Delzant subspace
\Delta:=\mu(S)\subset M
. There is a 1-1 correspondence between invariant Kähler metrics on
S
compatible with
\omega
and the following data.

(i)

A flat Lagrangian elliptic connection for
\mu:S\to M
with zero radial residue over the open facets of
\Delta
.

(ii)

A Hessian hybrid
b
-metric on
\Delta
with residues at the open facets given by the primitive outward-pointing normals multiplied by
\tfrac{1}{4\pi}
.

Moreover, an invariant Kähler metric
G
is extremal if and only if its scalar curvature
S_{G}
, viewed as function on
\Delta
, is affine.

Elliptic connections and hybrid
b
-metrics are tensors with specified singularities over the boundary of
\Delta
, introduced in Sections
4.5
and
7.1
, respectively. These are defined in a coordinate-free way using the elliptic tangent bundle of
\mu

[
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
]
and the
b
-tangent bundle of
\Delta

[
29
]
.

When
\Delta\subset M
is a finite type Delzant subspace and
M
carries a Hessian metric, our Delzant type construction above allows one to apply Kähler reduction, leading to the following result (see Section
7
).

Corollary
.

Let
(M,g)
be a connected integral affine Hessian manifold and
\Delta\subset M
a Delzant subspace of finite type with primitive boundary defining functions
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{d}
. The reduced Kähler metric on the symplectic quotient (
1.1
) induces the Hessian hybrid
b
-metric on
\Delta
given by

g_{\Delta}=g+\mathrm{Hess}_{\Lambda}(\phi),

where
\phi
is the smooth function on
\mathring{\Delta}
given by

\phi=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\ell_{i}\log|\ell_{i}|.

(1.2)

The relationship between our Delzant type construction and the standard Delzant construction will be explained in the main body of the paper. Here, we only point out that the metrics obtained by Kähler reduction via the two constructions do not quite coincide.

Our main theorem above provides an effective way of constructing (extremal) Kähler metrics in many examples, some of which have been obtained through much more elaborate means. In this paper, we only discuss a few illustrative examples, although we do show how to recover, by our techniques, the extremal Kähler metrics for a complex ruled surface over an elliptic curve that appears in the work of Apostolov et al.
[
2
]
– see Example
7.12
. More elaborate examples will appear in an upcoming joint paper with Miguel Abreu. We are further working on extensions of the theory developed here to contact structures, generalized complex structures and the non-commutative setting, i.e., multiplicity-free actions of proper symplectic groupoids.

Acknowledgments.
We would like to express our gratitude to Marius Crainic, Eugene Lerman, David Martínez Torres, Ioan Marcut, Daniele Sepe and Aldo Witte for the many inspiring discussions that
contributed to shaping the ideas presented in this paper. Special thanks are extended to Miguel Abreu, who provided invaluable advice on
this project and has become a collaborator. We also acknowledge the generous support of the Max Planck Institute for Mathematics in Bonn and the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign at various stages of this project.

Conventions and notations.
In this paper, for manifolds with corners we follow the conventions and terminology from
[
28
]
. For further background on manifolds with corners see, e.g.,
[
30
]
.

2.
Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces

In this sections we introduce Hamiltonian actions of symplectic torus bundles and discuss some basic properties.

2.1.
Symplectic torus bundles

In this paper, by a
torus bundle
we mean a fiber bundle
p:\mathcal{T}\to M
where:

•

each fiber is a Lie group isomorphic to a torus;

•

the fiberwise multiplication
m:\mathcal{T}\times_{M}\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}
, inversion
i:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}
and unit
u:M\to\mathcal{T}
are smooth maps.

On the other hand, an
equivalence
of torus bundles is a diffeomorphism

\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{M}

which restricts to a Lie group homomorphism on each fiber.

We will be specially interested in torus bundles carrying a symplectic form:

Definition 2.1
.

A
symplectic torus bundle
is a torus bundle
p:\mathcal{T}\to M
with a symplectic form
\Omega\in\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{T})
which is multiplicative, i.e., such that

m^{*}\Omega=\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}\Omega+\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}\Omega,

where
m,\operatorname{pr}_{1},\operatorname{pr}_{2}:\mathcal{T}\times_{M}\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}

are the fiberwise multiplication and the projections onto each factor, respectively. An
equivalence
of symplectic torus bundles is an equivalence of torus bundles preserving the symplectic forms.

The multiplicative condition forces the fibers of
p:\mathcal{T}\to M
and the unit section
u:M\to\mathcal{T}
to be Lagrangian submanifolds, so
\dim\mathcal{T}=2\dim M
.

Example 2.2
.

The simplest example of a symplectic torus bundle is

\operatorname{pr}:\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n},\qquad\Omega=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{d}\theta_{i}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{i},

where
(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n})
denote the angle coordinates on
\mathbb{T}^{n}:=\mathbb{R}^{n}/\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

Remark 2.3

(Symplectic groupoids)
.

The reader familiar with the groupoid language will recognize that a (symplectic) torus bundle is just a source-connected (symplectic) groupoid where the source and target maps coincide and multiplication is abelian.

Many of our results extend to the more general setting of symplectic groupoids. We will discuss this in future work. For now, we will sometimes use the language of symplectic groupoids in an auxiliary manner, but keeping the exposition self-contained. The curious reader can consult
[
13
]
for the basics of (symplectic) groupoids.

Given a symplectic torus bundle
p:(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
, the symplectic form allows to identify the Lie algebras of the fibers with the fibers of
T^{*}M
. Using this identification, the fiberwise exponential maps assembles to a surjective, symplectic, submersion

\exp:T^{*}M\to\mathcal{T},\quad\exp^{*}\Omega=\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}.

Since the identity section is Lagrangian, it follows that its preimage

\Lambda:=\exp^{-1}(M)\subset T^{*}M,

satisfies the two properties in the following definition.

Definition 2.4
.

A
integral affine structure
on a manifold
M
is a closed submanifold
\Lambda\subset T^{*}M
satisfying:

(i)

\Lambda
is a Lagrangian submanifold of
(T^{*}M,\Omega_{\mathrm{can}})
;

(ii)

\Lambda_{x}:=\Lambda\cap T_{x}^{*}M
is a full rank lattice for all
x\in M
.

Remark 2.5
.

One usually defines an integrable affine structure on a manifold
M
as a maximal atlas for which the coordinate changes are (restrictions of) maps of the form

x\mapsto Ax+b,\quad A\in\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{Z}),\ b\in\mathbb{R}.

Such atlas are in bijective correpondence with submanifolds
\Lambda\subset T^{*}M
as in Definition
2.4
. Given
\Lambda
one defines the integral affine charts to be any chart
(U,x^{1},\dots,x^{n})
such that

\Lambda|_{U}=\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{d}x^{1}+\cdots+\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{d}x^{n}.

Conversely, given such an integrable affine atlas the last expression defines
\Lambda
. Henceforth, we shall make use of this correspondence with no further notice. We refer to
[
14
,
4
,
23
]
for details on integral affine structures.

Hence, a symplectic torus bundle
p:(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
determines a canonical integral affine structure
\Lambda
on
M
and the exponential map induces an equivalence

T^{*}M/\Lambda\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\smash{$\sim$}}}\mathcal{T}.

This diffeomorphism pulls back
\Omega
to the unique symplectic form
{\Omega}_{\Lambda}\in\Omega^{2}(T^{*}M/\Lambda)

such that

q^{*}{\Omega}_{\Lambda}=\Omega_{\mathrm{can}},

where
q:T^{*}M\to T^{*}M/\Lambda
is the projection.

Conversely, given any integral affine structure
\Lambda
on a manifold
M
, we obtain a symplectic torus bundle
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
where

\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}:=T^{*}M/\Lambda,\quad q^{*}\Omega_{\Lambda}=\Omega_{\mathrm{can}},

and the projection
q:T^{*}M\to\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
becomes the exponential map. It follows that there is a 1:1 correspondence:

\left\{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-10.3611pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces symplectic torus bundles}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces over $M$}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces up to equivalence \,}\crcr}}}}\right\}\quad\tilde{\longleftrightarrow}\quad\left\{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-7.6919pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces integral affine structures}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces$\Lambda\subset T^{*}M$}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces\,}\crcr}}}}\right\}}}}}}}

Example 2.6
.

On
\mathbb{R}^{n}
we have the standard integral affine structure

\Lambda_{\mathrm{st}}=\mathbb{Z}\{\mathrm{d}x^{1},\dots,\mathrm{d}x^{n}\},

which corresponds to the trivial symplectic torus bundle

\operatorname{pr}_{2}:\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad\Omega_{\mathrm{st}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{d}\theta_{i}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{i}.

Similarly, on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}=\mathbb{R}^{n}/\mathbb{Z}^{n}
we have the integral affine structure

\Lambda_{\mathrm{st}}:=\mathbb{Z}\{\mathrm{d}x^{1},\dots,\mathrm{d}x^{n}\},

where now
x^{i}
are standard coordinates on the torus (so defined mod
\mathbb{Z}
). The corresponding symplectic torus bundle is

\operatorname{pr}_{2}:\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{T}^{n}\to\mathbb{T}^{n},\quad\Omega_{\mathrm{st}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{d}\theta_{i}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{i}.

Remark 2.7
.

Every symplectic torus
p:(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
is locally equivalent to the standard one
(\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Omega_{\mathrm{st}})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Indeed, an integral affine chart
(U,x^{i})
induces an isomorphism

\mathcal{T}|_{U}\simeq\mathbb{T}^{n}\times U,

which identifies
\Omega
and
\Omega_{\mathrm{st}}
.

Example 2.8
.

When
n=2
we have an exotic integral affine structure on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
defined by

\Lambda_{\mathrm{ex}}=\mathbb{Z}\{\mathrm{d}x^{1},\mathrm{d}x^{2}-x^{1}\mathrm{d}x^{1}\}.

The corresponding symplectic torus bundle can be described as follows. Consider the discrete subgroup
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{Aff}{Z}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
generated by the translations

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},x^{1},x^{2})=(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},x^{1}+1,x^{2}),

\displaystyle\quad\gamma_{2}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},x^{1},x^{2})=(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},x^{1},x^{2}+1),

\displaystyle\gamma_{3}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},x^{1},x^{2})=(\theta_{1}+1,\theta_{2},x^{1},x^{2}),

and by the element

\gamma_{4}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},x^{1},x^{2})=(\theta_{1}-x^{1},\theta_{2}+1,x^{1},x^{2}).

This group acts proper and free, by symplectic transformations of the canonical symplectic form

\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}=\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{1}+\mathrm{d}\theta_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}.

The sought after symplectic torus bundle has total space
\mathcal{T}:=\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma
, with symplectic form
\Omega
induced by
\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}
, and projection

p:\mathcal{T}\to\mathbb{T}^{2},\quad[\theta_{1},\theta_{2},x^{1},x^{2}]\mapsto[x^{1},x^{2}].

Indeed, this map has Lagrangian fibers and admits the Lagrangian section

s(x^{1},x^{2})=[0,0,x^{1},x^{2}].

This is the zero section for the multiplication

m([\theta_{1},\theta_{2},x^{1},x^{2}],[\bar{\theta}_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2},x^{1},x^{2}])=[\theta_{1}+\bar{\theta}_{1},\theta_{2}+\bar{\theta}_{2},x^{1},x^{2}].

Note that
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
is the Kodaira-Thurston symplectic manifold.

Let
p:(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
be a symplectic torus bundle inducing an integral affine structure
\Lambda\subset T^{*}M
. Then
M
carries a
canonical flat connection

\nabla
, namely the unique torsion free connection satisfying

\nabla_{\partial_{x^{i}}}\partial_{x^{j}}=0,

for any integral affine chart
(U,x^{i})
.
We will denote by the same symbol
\nabla
the dual connection on
T^{*}M
: it is the unique linear connection for which every local section of
\Lambda
is flat. The corresponding
horizontal distribution
will be denoted by
H^{\nabla}\subset T(T^{*}M)
.

At the level of the torus bundle
\mathcal{T}\to M
the existence of this flat connection is reflected in the existence of a Lagrangian foliation transverse to the fibers.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
p:(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
be a symplectic torus bundle inducing an integral affine structure
\Lambda\subset T^{*}M
. Then

H:=(\mathrm{d}\exp)(H^{\nabla})\subset T(\mathcal{T})

(2.1)

is a flat Ehresmann connection by Lagrangian subspaces, hence defines a Lagrangian foliation transverse to the fibers of
p:\mathcal{T}\to M
.

Proof.

Under the local trivialization induced by an integral affine chart (see Remark (
2.7
)), the exponential map
\exp:T^{*}M\to\mathcal{T}
is just the projection

\exp:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times U\to\mathbb{T}^{n}\times U,

and we find

H=(\mathrm{d}\exp)(H^{\nabla})=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\ker(\mathrm{d}\theta_{i}).

The statement now follows by observing that the forms
\mathrm{d}\theta_{i}
are multiplicative.
∎

Definition 2.10
.

The distribution
H\subset T(\mathcal{T})
given by (
2.1
)
is called the
canonical horizontal distribution
of the symplectic torus bundle.

Remark 2.11
.

The distribution
H
is an example of a
multiplicative Ehresmann connection
. In general, a distribution in a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is called
multiplicative
if it is a subgroupoid of the tangent groupoid
T(\mathcal{G})
. We refer to
[
21
,
22
]
for more details on multiplicative Ehresmann connections.

2.2.
Morphisms of symplectic torus bundles

We will make use of the following notion of morphism of symplectic torus bundles.

Definition 2.12
.

A
morphism
of symplectic torus bundles

(\phi,\Phi):(\mathcal{T}_{1},\Omega_{1})\dashrightarrow(\mathcal{T}_{2},\Omega_{2})

consists of a map
\phi:M_{1}\to M_{2}
between the basis and a bundle map

\textstyle{\phi^{*}\mathcal{T}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{pr}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{M_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{M_{1}}

satisfying:

(i)

\Phi
restricts on each fiber to a group morphism;

(ii)

\mathrm{Graph}(\Phi)\subset\overline{\mathcal{T}_{1}}\times\mathcal{T}_{2}
is a Lagrangian submanifold.

The graph in the last item in the previous definition is the relation defined by:

\mathrm{Graph}(\Phi):=\{(\Phi(g,x),g):(g,x)\in\mathcal{T}_{2}\times_{M_{1}}M_{1}\}.

The notation
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
means, as usual, the symplectic manifold
\mathcal{T}
with the opposite symplectic structure.

Remark 2.13
.

The notion of morphism in the previous definition is actually an instance of the notion of a groupoid comorphism
[
8
]
. Since we do not consider groupoid morphisms or other notions of morphism for torus bundles, we will use the term “morphism”.

The notion of morphism of symplectic torus bundles is intimately related to the concept of integral affine map which we now recall (see
[
14
,
4
,
23
]
for more details).

Definition 2.14
.

Let
(M_{1},\Lambda_{1})
and
(M_{2},\Lambda_{2})
be integral affine manifolds. A map

\phi:(M_{1},\Lambda_{1})\to(M_{2},\Lambda_{2})

(2.2)

is called an
integral affine map
if
\phi^{*}(\Lambda_{2})\subset\Lambda_{1}
. It is called a
primitive integral affine map
if
\phi^{*}(\Lambda_{2})=\Lambda_{1}
.

Remark 2.15
.

A map
\phi:(M_{1},\Lambda_{1})\to(M_{2},\Lambda_{2})
is integral affine if and only if in integral affine charts (with connected domains) it is of the form

x\mapsto Ax+b,

for some linear integral affine map
A:(\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}},\mathbb{Z}^{m_{1}})\to(\mathbb{R}^{m_{2}},\mathbb{Z}^{m_{2}})
and some
b\in\mathbb{R}
.

The next proposition shows that the category
\mathbf{IntAffMan}
of integral affine manifolds is equivalent to the category
\mathbf{SympTorBun}
.

Proposition 2.16
.

The assignment
(M,\Lambda)\mapsto(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
extends to a functor

\mathbf{IntAffMan}\to\mathbf{SympTorBun}

by assigning to an integral affine map
\phi
as in (
2.2
) the morphism

(\phi,\underline{\phi}^{*}):(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{1}},\Omega_{\Lambda_{1}})\dashrightarrow(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{2}},\Omega_{\Lambda_{2}}),\quad\underline{\phi}^{*}([\alpha],x)=[(\mathrm{d}_{x}\phi)^{*}\alpha].

This functor is in fact an equivalence of categories.

Proof.

By the remark above the map
\underline{\phi}^{*}
is well-defined. It is readily verified that
(\phi,\underline{\phi}^{*})
is a morphism, and that this construction defines a functor. To see that this is an equivalence of categories, let
(\phi,\Phi):(\mathcal{T}_{1},\Omega_{1})\dashrightarrow(\mathcal{T}_{2},\Omega_{2})
be any morphism between symplectic torus bundles. Then
\Phi
has a unique lift to a vector bundle map

\textstyle{\phi^{*}T^{*}M_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\Phi}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}\times\exp}
\textstyle{T^{*}M_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exp}
\textstyle{\phi^{*}\mathcal{T}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{1}}

whose graph

\mathrm{Graph}(\widetilde{\Phi})\subset\overline{T^{*}M_{1}}\times T^{*}M_{2}

is a Lagrangian submanifold. One checks (e.g., by working in local coordinates) that this last condition implies that

\widetilde{\Phi}=\phi^{*}.

The commutativity of the diagram then forces

\phi^{*}(\Lambda_{2})\subset\Lambda_{1},

where
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
are the integral affine structures induced by
(\mathcal{T}_{1},\Omega_{1})
and
(\mathcal{T}_{2},\Omega_{2})
. By the remark above, we conclude that
\phi
is an integral affine map.
∎

2.3.
Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces

One of our main objects of study are Hamiltonian spaces for symplectic torus bundles. An
action of a torus bundle

p:\mathcal{T}\to M
on map
\mu:S\to M
is given by a map

\mathbscr{A}:\mathcal{T}\times_{M}S\to S,\quad(g,p)\mapsto g\cdot p,

satisfying the usual properties:

(i)

g\cdot(h\cdot p)=(gh)\cdot p
;

(ii)

u(\mu(p))\cdot p=p
.

In other words, this is just a smooth varying fiberwise action.

Symplectic torus bundles act on maps whose domain is a symplectic manifold:

Definition 2.17
.

Let
p:(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
be a symplectic torus bundle. A
Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
consists of:

(i)

a symplectic manifold
(S,\omega)
;

(ii)

a map
\mu:S\to M
;

(iii)

an action
\mathbscr{A}:\mathcal{T}\times_{M}S\to S
which is symplectic, i.e., that satisfies

\mathbscr{A}^{*}\omega=\operatorname{pr}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\Omega+\operatorname{pr}_{S}^{*}\omega.

Since a Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space consists fiberwise of ordinary abelian actions, we obtain an
infinitesimal action
of the bundle of abelian Lie algebras
T^{*}M\to M
on
\mu:S\to M
. In other words, we have a linear map
\mathbscr{a}:\Omega^{1}(M)\to\mathfrak{X}(S)
satisfying

\mu_{*}(\mathbscr{a}(\alpha))=0,\qquad[\mathbscr{a}(\alpha),\mathbscr{a}(\beta)]=0,\quad(\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{1}(M)).

We call
\mu:S\to M
the
moment map
of the action and it satisfies the moment map condition (see
[
13
]
)

i_{\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)}\omega=\mu^{*}\alpha\quad(\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(M)).

(2.3)

Example 2.18

(Classical Hamiltonian spaces)
.

A classical Hamiltonian
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-space with moment map
\mu:(S,\omega)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the same thing as Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space for the symplectic torus bundle
\mathcal{T}:=(\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Omega_{\mathrm{can}})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The
\mathcal{T}
-action and is given in terms of the
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-action determine each other by

\mathbscr{A}:(\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})\times_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}S\to S,\ (g,x,p)\mapsto gp,\quad\text{ if }\quad x=\mu(p).

Conversely, any
\mathcal{T}
-action takes this form for a unique
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-action.

Example 2.19

(The trivial Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space)
.

Let
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
be any symplectic torus bundle. Then
\mathcal{T}
acts on itself by left translations

\mathbscr{A}:\mathcal{T}\times_{M}\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T},(g,h)\mapsto gh,

with moment map the projection
p:\mathcal{T}\to M
. The multiplicativity of
\Omega
shows that this is a Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space.

Example 2.20

(The trivial
\mathbb{S}^{2}
-bundle over
\mathbb{T}^{2}
)
.

Consider the symplectic manifold

(S,\omega):=(\mathbb{T}^{2},\omega_{\mathbb{T}^{2}})\times(\mathbb{S}^{2},\omega_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}),

where
\omega_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}
and
\omega_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
denote the area forms with total area 1, so

\omega_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}:=\mathrm{d}y\wedge\mathrm{d}x,\quad\omega_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}:=\mathrm{d}\phi\wedge\mathrm{d}h,

where
(\phi,h)
are cylindrical coordinates on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
This admits an action of the symplectic torus bundle
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
corresponding to the standard integral affine cylinder

(M,\Lambda):=(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{d}x\oplus\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{d}h).

Explicitly, the symplectic torus bundle

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T},\Omega)

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R},\mathrm{d}y\wedge\mathrm{d}x+\mathrm{d}\theta\wedge\mathrm{d}h),

\displaystyle p:\mathcal{T}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R},\quad(y,\theta,x,h)\mapsto(x,h),

acts along the map

\mu:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R},(y,x,\phi,h)\to(x,h),

as follows

(y,\theta,x,h)\cdot(\tilde{y},x,\phi,h)=(y+\tilde{y},x,\theta+\phi,h).

Example 2.21

(The non-trivial
\mathbb{S}^{2}
-bundle over
\mathbb{T}^{2}
)
.

The non-trivial orientable
\mathbb{S}^{2}
-bundle over
\mathbb{T}^{2}
can be obtained by taking the quotient of the trivial
\mathbb{S}^{2}
-bundle
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
by the proper and free action of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
given by

(k_{1},k_{2})\cdot(y,x,\phi,h)=(y+k_{1},x+k_{2},\phi-k_{2}y,h).

Let
(S,\omega)
be the resulting manifold
\mathbb{T}^{2}\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.95134pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\cr$\times$\cr}}}\mathbb{S}^{2}:=(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2})/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
equipped with the symplectic form induced by the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-invariant symplectic form

(h+2)\mathrm{d}y\wedge\mathrm{d}x+(x\mathrm{d}y+\mathrm{d}\phi)\wedge\mathrm{d}h.

This symplectic manifold admits an action of the symplectic torus bundle
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
associated to the integral affine manifold

(M,\Lambda)=(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times]-2,\infty[,\mathbb{Z}((h+2)\mathrm{d}x+x\mathrm{d}h)\oplus\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{d}h).

Explicitly, this torus bundle is

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}\times]-2,\infty[\right)/{\mathbb{Z}},

\displaystyle p:\mathcal{T}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}\times]-2,\infty[,\quad(y,\theta,x,h)\mapsto(x,h),

where
\mathbb{Z}
acts as

k\cdot(y,\theta,x,h)=(y,\theta-ky,x+k,h),

and the multiplication on the fibers is just addition. The symplectic form
\Omega
is induced by the
\mathbb{Z}
-invariant symplectic form

(h+2)\mathrm{d}y\wedge\mathrm{d}x+(x\mathrm{d}y+\mathrm{d}\theta)\wedge\mathrm{d}h.

The symplectic torus bundle
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
acts on
(S,\omega)
along the map

\mu:\mathbb{T}^{2}\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.95134pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\cr$\times$\cr}}}\mathbb{S}^{2}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}\times]-2,\infty[,\quad(y,x,\phi,h)\mapsto(x,h),

as follows

(y,\theta,x,h)\cdot(\tilde{y},x,\phi,h)=(y+\tilde{y},x,\theta+\phi,h).

Let
\mu_{1}:(S_{1},\omega_{1})\to M
and
\mu_{2}:(S_{2},\omega_{2})\to M
be Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces. Then a morphism from
\mu_{1}
to
\mu_{2}
is a symplectic map

\textstyle{S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{1}}
\textstyle{S_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{2}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{M}

which is
\mathcal{T}
-equivariant:

\Psi(g\cdot p)=g\cdot\Psi(p).

We need a generalization of this notion to Hamiltonian spaces of distinct symplectic torus bundles.

Definition 2.22
.

Let
(\phi,\Phi):(\mathcal{T}_{1},\Omega_{1})\dashrightarrow(\mathcal{T}_{2},\Omega_{2})
be a morphism of symplectic torus bundles, and
let
\mu_{i}:(S_{i},\omega_{i})\to M_{i}
be Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}_{i}
-spaces. A
morphism
from
\mu_{1}
to
\mu_{2}
covering
(\phi,\Phi)
is a map

\textstyle{S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{1}}
\textstyle{S_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{2}}
\textstyle{M_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{M_{2}}

satisfying:

(i)

\Psi
is a Poisson map;

(ii)

\Psi
is
(\phi,\Phi)
-equivariant, i.e.,

\Psi(\Phi(g,\mu_{1}(p))\cdot p)=g\cdot\Psi(p)
.

The base map of a morphism of Hamiltonian spaces is an integral affine map by Proposition
2.16
. For an
isomorphism
this map must be an integral affine isomorphism, while the the map
\Psi
between the total spaces must be a symplectomorphism. An isomorphism for which the base map
\phi
is the identity, and so
\mathcal{T}_{1}=\mathcal{T}_{2}
, will be called an
equivalence
.

Example 2.23
.

Let
\mu:(S,\omega)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Hamiltonian
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-space and
K_{\theta}:\mathbb{T}^{m}\to\mathbb{T}^{n}
a Lie group homomorphism. The induced Lie algebra homomorphism
\theta:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a linear integral affine map
(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{Z}^{m})\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{Z}^{n})
, and we have a morphism of symplectic torus bundles

(\phi,\Phi):\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{T}^{m},\phi=\theta^{*},\ \Phi(g,x)=(x,K_{\theta}(g)).

On the other hand, the induced
\mathbb{T}^{m}
-action on
S

h\cdot x:=K_{\theta}(h)\cdot x,

is Hamiltonian, with momentum map
\tilde{\mu}:=\theta^{*}\circ\mu:S\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Moreover,

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\mu}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{m}}

is a a morphism of Hamiltonian spaces covering
(\phi,\Phi)
.

2.4.
\mathcal{T}
-invariant tensors

Let
p:(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
be a symplectic torus bundle. Given a Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
, the torus bundle
\mathcal{T}
acts on the
\mu
-fibers so each
g\in\mathcal{T}
induces a map between the tangent spaces to the fibers:

g_{*}:T\mu^{-1}(x)\to T\mu^{-1}(x),\text{ with }x=p(g).

In order to extend the action of
g
to the tangent bundle
TS
we use the canonical horizontal distribution
H
(see Definition
2.10
). We denote the corresponding horizontal lift of tangent vectors by:

\operatorname{hor}_{g}:T_{x}M\to T_{g}\mathcal{T},\quad\operatorname{hor}_{g}(w):=(\mathrm{d}_{g}p)|_{H}^{-1}(w).

Definition 2.24
.

Given a symplectic torus bundle
p:(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
and a Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
the
tangent action
of
g\in\mathcal{T}
is the map

g_{*}:T_{p}S\to T_{gp}S,\quad g_{*}v:=\mathrm{d}\mathbscr{A}(\operatorname{hor}_{g}(\mathrm{d}\mu(v)),v).

The multiplicativity of the distribution
H
ensures that this defines an action of the torus bundle
\mathcal{T}
on the map
\mu\circ\operatorname{pr}:TS\to M
.

Remark 2.25
.

The distribution
H
is an example of a Cartan connection on the torus bundle
p:\mathcal{T}\to M
. The construction of the action in Definition
2.24
is a special case of a more general construction valid for actions of Lie groupoids
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
on a map
\mu:S\to M
. Whenever the Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is equipped with a Cartan connection one obtains an action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mu\circ\operatorname{pr}:TS\to M
(see, e.g.,
[
3
]
)

Definition 2.26
.

We say that a tensor field on the total space of a Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
is
\mathcal{T}
-invariant
if it is invariant under the tangent action of every
g\in\mathcal{T}
.

Similarly, one can talk about
\mathcal{T}
-invariance of other objects. For example, a distribution
D\subset TS
is
\mathcal{T}
-invariant if is preserved under the tangent action. A foliation of
S
is
\mathcal{T}
-invariant if the corresponding distribution if
\mathcal{T}
-invariant.

Example 2.27
.

For any symplectic torus bundle
p:(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
the symplectic form
\Omega
and the horizontal distribution
H
are both
\mathcal{T}
-invariant. For any Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
the symplectic form
\omega
is also
\mathcal{T}
-invariant.

Given a Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
, fixing a integral affine chart
(U,x^{i})
, one obtains a
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-action on
S|_{U}
. This follows from the fact that a choice of integral affine chart induces a local trivialization of the symplectic torus bundle (cf. Remark
2.7
). Although these locally defined
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-actions depend on the choice of integral affine chart, it turns out that
\mathcal{T}
-invariance is equivalent to
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-invariance, as stated in the next proposition. For the statement, we will the fact that a local section
s:U\to\mathcal{T}
of
\mathcal{T}\to M
acts on
S|_{U}
as thefiber preserving diffeomorphism

p\mapsto s(\mu(p))\cdot p.

Proposition 2.28
.

Given a tensor field
K
on the total space of a Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
the following are equivalent:

(i)

K
is
\mathcal{T}
-invariant;

(ii)

K
is invariant under the action of any horizontal local section of
\mathcal{T}
;

(iii)

K
is invariant under the infinitesimal action of any locally defined flat 1-form

\mathscr{L}_{\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)}K=0\text{ if }\nabla\alpha=0;

(2.4)

(iv)

K
is
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-invariant relative to the locally defined
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-action induced by any integral affine chart.

Proof.

Obviously, a tensor is
\mathcal{T}
-invariant if and only if it is
\mathcal{T}|_{U_{a}}
-invariant for some open cover
\{U_{a}\}
of
M
. Hence, it is enough to check the statement on an integral affine chart
(U,x^{i})
with connected domain.

The equivalence of (i) and (ii) follows from the fact that any element
g\in\mathcal{T}
extends to a horizontal local section (see proof of Proposition
2.9
).

For the equivalence between (ii) and (iii), observe that if
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(U)
is any flat 1-form then
\exp(t\alpha):U\to\mathcal{T}
is a horizontal section, and acting by this local section is given by the flow of the vector field
\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)\in\mathfrak{X}(S)
(cf. (
2.3
))

\mathbscr{A}(\exp(t\alpha),p)=\phi^{t}_{\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)}(p).

For the equivalence between (ii) and (iv), observe that the choice of integral affine chart gives an identification

\mathbb{T}^{n}\times U\simeq\mathcal{T}|_{U}.

Under this identification, the horizontal sections of
\mathcal{T}
become the constant sections of
\mathbb{T}^{n}\times U\to U
. Therefore, the action by
g\in\mathbb{T}^{n}
coincides with the action by the horizontal section
x\mapsto(g,x)
, and the equivalence follows.
∎

3.
Principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces a.k.a. Lagrangian fibrations

A very important class of Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces are
Lagrangian fibrations
. In this section we show that they correspond exactly to principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces and we recall their classification, from this point of view.

3.1.
Lagrangian fibrations

By a
Lagrangian fibration
we mean the following.

Definition 3.1
.

A
Lagrangian fibration
is a symplectic manifold
(S,\omega)
together with a surjective submersion
\mu:S\to M
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

\dim(S)=2\dim(M)
;

(ii)

\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
is a Poisson map, where
M
is equipped with the zero Poisson structure;

(iii)

\mu
is a proper with connected fibers.

Notice that conditions (i) and (ii) can be replaced by the simpler statement that the fibers are Lagrangian submanifolds. We prefer this formulation since it is more natural from the perspective of Poisson geometry adopted here, specially having in mind generalizations to the non-commutative setting.

Given any Lagrangian fibration
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
one obtains a symplectic torus bundle
p:(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
which acts in a Hamiltonian fashion on
\mu
as follows. First, the Lagrangian fibration induces an integral affine structure
\Lambda
on
M
given by

\Lambda:=\{\alpha\in T^{*}M:\phi^{1}_{\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)}=\mathrm{Id}\}.

Here, for
\alpha\in T^{*}_{x}M
we denoted by
\phi^{1}_{\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)}
the time-1 flow of the vector field
\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)
on the fiber
\mu^{-1}(x)
defined by (
2.3
). Then the symplectic torus bundle
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
associated with
\Lambda
acts on the fibration by setting:

[\alpha]\cdot p:=\phi_{\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)}^{1}(p)\quad(\alpha\in T_{\mu(p)}^{*}M).

One checks that this is indeed a Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-action (see, e.g.,
[
13
, Chp. 12]
). Moreover, this is a
principal action
, i.e., the map

\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}\times_{M}S\to S\times_{M}S,\quad(g,p)\mapsto(gp,p),

is a diffeomorphism.

Conversely, given a symplectic torus bundle
p:(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
, every
principal
Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
is a Lagrangian fibration. In other words:

\left\{\\
\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-6.94444pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces principal Hamiltonian $\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}$-spaces}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces$\mu:(S,\omega)\to M$}\crcr}}}}\right\}\ =\ \left\{\\
\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-6.94444pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces Lagrangian fibrations}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces$\mu:(S,\omega)\to M$ inducing $\Lambda$}\crcr}}}}\right\}}}}}

Two Lagrangian fibrations
\mu_{i}:(S_{i},\omega_{i})\to M
are called
equivalent
if there exists a symplectomorphism
\Psi:(S_{1},\omega_{1})\to(S_{2},\omega_{2})
commuting with the projections:

\textstyle{S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{1}}
\textstyle{S_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{2}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{M}

It is easy to see that equivalent Lagrangian fibrations induce the same integral affine structure
\Lambda\subset T^{*}M
and
\Psi
is an equivalence of Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-spaces.

Example 3.2

(Symplectic torus bundles)
.

A symplectic torus bundle
p:(\mathcal{T},\omega)\to M
is an example of a Lagrangian fibration satisfying a very special property: it admits a global Lagrangian section, namely the identity section. Conversely, if a principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
admits a global Lagrangian section
s:M\to S
then we obtain an equivalence

(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.68887pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\smash{$\sim$}}}(S,\omega),\quad g\mapsto g\cdot s(p(g)).

Under this equivalence, the
\mathcal{T}
-action on
S
becomes the action of
\mathcal{T}
on itself by translations.

In general, a Lagrangian fibration does not admit a global Lagrangian section. However, locally such sections always exists, and this gives a local model for a Lagrangian fibrations, as discussed in the next example.

Remark 3.3

(Action-angle coordinates)
.

Let
p:(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
be a symplectic torus bundle. Any principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
is locally equivalent to
p:(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
. Namely, choosing an open set
U\subset M
where there exists a Lagrangian section
s:U\to S
of
\mu
, we obtain an equivalence as in the previous example

\Psi:\mathcal{T}|_{U}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.68887pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\smash{$\sim$}}}S|_{U},\quad g\mapsto g\cdot s(p(g)).

It follows also from the previous discussion that any two Lagrangian fibrations inducing the same integral affine structure
\Lambda
are locally equivalent.

Moreover, if we fix integral affine coordinates
(U,x^{i})
over which there exists a Lagrangian section
s:U\to S
of
\mu
, we obtain an equivalence (cf. Remark (
2.7
))

S|_{U}\simeq\mathbb{T}^{n}\times U,\quad\omega|_{U}\simeq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{d}\theta_{i}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{i}.

This local form only depends on the choice of an integral affine chart and a Lagrangian section. This is sometimes referred to as the Arnold-Liouville Theorem, and the coordinates
(x^{i},\theta_{i})
are called action-angle coordinates.

3.2.
Classification of principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces

The obstruction for a Lagrangian fibration to admit a global Lagrangian section is given by the
Lagrangian-Chern class
, as we now recall.
This class also yields Duistermaat’s global classification of Lagrangian fibrations
[
19
]
. In our language, it allows to classify principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces, up to equivalence.

Given a symplectic torus bundle
p:(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
denote by
\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}}
its sheaf of Lagrangian sections. The groupoid multiplication makes this into an abelian sheaf. Given a principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
, the usual construction of the Chern class using Čech cocycles can be performed using local
Lagrangian
sections. This yields a Čech cohomology class

c_{1}(S,\omega)\in\check{H}^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}}),

called the
Lagrangian-Chern class
.

Theorem 3.4

(Duistermaat)
.

Given a symplectic torus bundle
p:(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
the Lagrangian-Chern class gives a 1:1 correspondence:

c_{1}:\left\{\\
\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-11.94444pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces principal Hamiltonian $\mathcal{T}$-spaces}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces$\mu:(S,\omega)\to M$}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces up to equivalence\,}\crcr}}}}\right\}\ \tilde{\longrightarrow}\ \check{H}^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})}}}

Since
\check{H}^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})
is an abelian group, one also has an abelian group structure on the equivalence classes of principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces. This group structure can be described explicitly using the fusion product of principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces (see, e.g.,
[
14
]
).

An integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
is called
complete
if the canonical flat connection
\nabla
is complete. Lagrangian fibrations over a complete integral affine manifold have the following simple description.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
(M,\Lambda)
be a complete, integral affine manifold so
M\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n}/\Gamma
. There is a 1:1 correspondence:

\left\{\\
\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-6.94444pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces principal Hamiltonian $\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}$-spaces}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces$\mu:(S,\omega)\to M$\,}\crcr}}}}\right\}\ \tilde{\longleftrightarrow}\ \left\{\\
\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-11.88889pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces symplectic actions}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces$\Gamma\curvearrowright(\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\omega_{\mathrm{can}})$}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces covering $\Gamma\curvearrowright\mathbb{R}^{n}$\,}\crcr}}}}\right\}}}}}}

Proof.

Since
(M,\Lambda)
is complete, we have
M\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n}/\Gamma
, with
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(M)\subset\operatorname{Aff}{Z}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
acting properly and free on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Assume
\Gamma\curvearrowright(\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\omega_{\mathrm{can}})
is a symplectic action covering
\Gamma\curvearrowright\mathbb{R}^{n}
. It is a proper and free action, and the induced symplectic form on the quotient

S:=(\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})/\Gamma\to M=\mathbb{R}^{n}/\Gamma,

yields a Lagrangian fibration with base the integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
. So it is a principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-space.

Conversely, given a principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
we consider its pullback under the covering projection
q:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to M

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 11.55557pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.77779pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\widetilde{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-11.55557pt\raise-15.76389pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.61111pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\tilde{\mu}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.19446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.19849pt\raise 6.61111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.61111pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\tilde{q}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 35.5984pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 35.5984pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.95256pt\raise-15.76389pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mu}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.95256pt\raise-24.19446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-9.55673pt\raise-31.52777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 16.01811pt\raise-26.34027pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{q}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.55673pt\raise-31.52777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 33.55673pt\raise-31.52777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Here
\tilde{q}:\widetilde{S}\to S
is also a
\Gamma
-covering where
\Gamma
acts on the first factor of
\widetilde{S}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times_{M}S
. This action makes
\tilde{\mu}
equivariant and preserves the pullback symplectic

\tilde{\omega}:=\tilde{q}^{*}\omega.

On the other hand,
\tilde{\mu}:(\widetilde{S},\tilde{\omega})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}

is a principal Hamiltonian
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-space. Any such space is isomorphic to the canonical Hamiltonian
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-space
(\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\omega_{\mathrm{can}})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, so the result follows.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

The previous result can be related to Duistermaat classification Theorem
3.4
as follows.
On the one hand, under the assumptions of Proposition
3.5
,one has an identification:

\check{H}^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})\simeq\check{H}^{1}_{\Gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\textrm{Lag}}})

where the right-hand side denotes
\Gamma
-equivariant cohomology for the
\Gamma
-sheaf of Lagrangian sections of
(\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\omega_{\mathrm{can}})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

On the other hand, one has a
\Gamma
-equivariant version of Duistermaat result, which gives a 1:1 correspondence:

\left\{\\
\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-11.33333pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces$\Gamma$-equivariant }\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces principal
Hamiltonian $\mathbb{T}^{n}$-spaces}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces$\tilde{\mu}:(\widetilde{S},\tilde{\omega})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}$, up to isomorphism \,}\crcr}}}}\right\}\ \tilde{\longrightarrow}\ \check{H}^{1}_{\Gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\textrm{Lag}}})}}}

Any such Hamiltonian space admits a global Lagrangian section and so is symplectomorphic to the canonical bundle
\operatorname{pr}:(\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\omega_{\mathrm{can}})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, recovering the correspondence in the proposition.

Notice that the induced symplectic action of
\Gamma
on
\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, in general, does not coincide with the canonical lift of the action
\Gamma\curvearrowright\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This happens precisely when
\tilde{\mu}:(\widetilde{S},\tilde{\omega})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
admits a
\Gamma
-invariant Lagrangian section, i.e., when it is represented by the trivial class in
\check{H}^{1}_{\Gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\textrm{Lag}}})
.

Proposition
3.5
is also useful to construct examples. We illustrate it with one such example.

Example 3.7
.

The standard integral affine structure on the torus
M=\mathbb{R}^{2}/\Gamma
is obtained from the action of
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by translations:

(m,n)\cdot(x^{1},x^{2})=(x^{1}+m,x^{2}+n).

The canonical lift
\Gamma\curvearrowright\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
is given by

(m,n)\cdot(x^{1},x^{2},\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=(x^{1}+m,x^{2}+n,\theta_{1},\theta_{2}).

The zero section is a
\Gamma
-equivariant Lagrangian section and this agrees with the fact that the corresponding quotient is the trivial bundle
(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{2},\omega_{\mathrm{can}})\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
, which has representative the trivial class in
\check{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})

More generally, fix
a\in[0,1[
and consider the symplectic action
\Gamma\curvearrowright\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
covering
\Gamma\curvearrowright\mathbb{R}^{2}
given by

(m,n)\cdot(x^{1},x^{2},\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=(x^{1}+m,x^{2}+n,\theta_{1}+an,\theta_{2}).

(3.1)

For this action, the bundle
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
still admits a
\Gamma
-equivariant section, namely

s(x^{1},x^{2})=(x^{1},x^{2},-ax^{2},0).

Hence, we obtain a family of Lagrangian fibrations, each isomorphic to the trivial fibration
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
, but with a new symplectic form

\omega_{a}=\omega_{\mathrm{can}}+a\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}.

Notice that the integral affine structure induced on the base
\mathbb{T}^{2}
is still the standard one.
If
s:\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
is any section,
s^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{can}}
is an exact form and applying Stokes formula we find that

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}s^{*}\omega_{a}=a\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}=a.

Therefore, if
a\in]0,1[
the fibration
(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{2},\omega_{a})\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
does not admit any Lagrangian section. Equivalently, there is no
\Gamma
-equivariant section of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
which is Lagrangian (this can also be checked directly). This gives examples of Lagrangian fibrations with trivial Chern class and non-trivial Lagrangian-Chern class
c(S,\omega)\in\check{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})
.

3.3.
Lagrangian connections

In the construction of the Chern class of a principal torus bundle one passes from
integral to real coefficients using connections. One has a similar situation for principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces, for which the concept of a
Lagrangian connection
is needed.

Definition 3.8
.

A
connection
for a principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
is a
\mathcal{T}
-invariant distribution
D\subset TS
such that
TS=D\oplus\ker(\mathrm{d}\mu)
. We call such a connection
Lagrangian
if
D_{p}\subset(T_{p}S,\omega_{p})
is Lagrangian for all
p\in S
.

Example 3.9
.

For
p:(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
, viewed as a principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space with the action by translations, the canonical horizontal distribution (see Definition
2.10
) is a Lagrangian connection.

Example 3.10
.

Let
\mu:(S,\omega)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a classical Hamiltonian
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-space, viewed as Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space for the symplectic torus bundle
\mathcal{T}=(\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Omega_{\mathrm{can}})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
. If the action is principal, then a connection in the sense of Definition
3.8
is just an ordinary principal bundle connection. It is Lagrangian if and only if its horizontal spaces are Lagrangian.

Connections can also be described by
connection
1
-forms
, which are defined as follows. For a Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
and a vector bundle
E\to M
we denote by
\Omega^{k}(S,E)
the space of
k
-forms with values in
\mu^{*}E
.

Definition 3.11
.

A
connection 1-form
for a principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
is a form
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(S,T^{*}M)
which satisfies:

(i)

\mathcal{T}
-invariance:
\theta_{gp}(g\cdot v)=\theta_{p}(v)
, for all
v\in T_{p}S
,
g\in\mathcal{T}_{\mu(p)}
;

(ii)

\theta_{p}(\mathbscr{a}_{p}(\alpha))=\alpha
, for all
\alpha\in T_{\mu(p)}^{*}M
and
p\in S
.

Note that in the previous definition, in (i) we have used the tangent action of
\mathcal{T}
on
S
and in (ii) its infinitesimal version
\mathbscr{a}:\mu^{*}T^{*}M\to TS
.

Similar to ordinary principal connections, there is a bijective correspondence between connections and connection
1
-forms for a principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
, in which the distribution corresponding to a connection
1
-form
\theta
is
D=\ker\theta
. The following lemma characterizes those connection 1-forms giving rise to Lagrangian connections.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
\theta
be a connection
1
-form for a principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
. Consider the
\mathcal{T}
-invariant, non-degenerate,
2
-form
\omega_{\theta}\in\Omega^{2}(S)
defined by

\omega_{\theta}(v,w):=\langle\theta(v),\mathrm{d}\mu(w)\rangle-\langle\theta(w),\mathrm{d}\mu(v)\rangle.

The connection
\ker\theta
is Lagrangian if and only if
\omega=\omega_{\theta}
.

Proof.

The backward direction is immediate. For the forward direction, note that
\operatorname{Im}(\mathbscr{a})=\ker(\mathrm{d}\mu)
by principality of the action. So, it suffices to verify the equality
\omega=\omega_{\theta}
on tangent vectors of the form
v+\mathbscr{a}_{p}(\alpha)
, with
v\in\ker(\theta_{p})
and
\alpha\in T^{*}_{\mu(p)}M
. This is readily done using the moment map condition (
2.3
).
∎

Proposition 3.13
.

Every principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space admits a Lagrangian connection.

Proof.

Remark
3.3
and Example
3.9
show that over a sufficiently small integral affine chart a Lagrangian connection always exists. Consider an open cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
M
together with connection
1
-forms
\theta_{i}
for
\mu:S|_{U_{i}}\to U_{i}
, such that
\ker(\theta_{i})
is Lagrangian. Choosing a partition of unity
\{\rho_{i}\}_{i\in I}
subordinated to this cover, we can construct a global connection
1
-form
\theta:=\sum_{i\in I}(\rho_{i}\circ\mu)\theta_{i}
. In view of the lemma above,
\ker(\theta)
is Lagrangian, since

\omega_{\theta}=\sum_{i\in I}(\rho_{i}\circ\mu)\omega_{\theta_{i}}=\sum_{i\in I}(\rho_{i}\circ\mu)\omega=\omega.

∎

Next we define the curvature 2-form of a connection on a principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
. Pulling back the canonical flat connection
\nabla
on
T^{*}M
along the projection
\mu:S\to M
we obtain a flat connection
\nabla
on
\mu^{*}T^{*}M
and this gives a differential
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}:\Omega^{\bullet}(S,T^{*}M)\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}(S,T^{*}M)
. We further denote by
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\partial}(M,T^{*}M)
the kernel of the anti-symmetrization map

\partial:\Omega^{\bullet}(M,T^{*}M)\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}(M),

(3.2)

which is a subcomplex of

(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,T^{*}M),\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}).

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
D
be a connection on
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
with connection 1-form
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(S,T^{*}M)
. There is a unique 2-form
K_{\theta}\in\Omega^{2}(M,T^{*}M)
such that

\mu^{*}K_{\theta}=\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\theta.

This satisfies the following properties:

(i)

K_{\theta}
is
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}
-closed;

(ii)

K_{\theta}=0
if and only if
D
is involutive.

Moreover, if
D
is a Lagrangian connection then
K_{\theta}\in\Omega^{2}_{\partial}(M,T^{*}M)
, meaning that

\partial K_{\theta}(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}):=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{3}}(-1)^{|\sigma|}\langle K_{\theta}(v_{\sigma(1)},v_{\sigma(2)}),v_{\sigma(3)}\rangle=0.

Proof.

The existence of
K_{\theta}
follows from the remark that
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\theta
is horizontal and
\mathcal{T}
-invariant, and its uniqueness is clear. Properties (i) and (ii) are immediate, whilst the last property follows by observing that

\mathrm{d}\omega_{\theta}=\frac{1}{2}\mu^{*}(\partial K_{\theta}),

so that (by Lemma
3.12
)
\partial K_{\theta}
must vanish if
\theta
is Lagrangian.
∎

According to the previous proposition, a choice of Lagrangian connection on
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
gives rise to a cohomology class

[K_{\theta}]\in H^{2}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\partial}(M,T^{*}M),\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}).

This class is independent of the choice of Lagrangian connection and, as for principal
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-bundles, it is related to the Lagrangian Chern class by a map that ‘passes from integer to real coefficients’. To make this precise, consider the composite

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 20.63892pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.63892pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\check{H}^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.20143pt\raise 4.72855pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.72855pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\simeq}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 35.63892pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 35.63892pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\check{H}^{2}(M,\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 100.66681pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 100.66681pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\check{H}^{2}(M,\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{Aff}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 157.45862pt\raise 4.72855pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.72855pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\simeq}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 171.08362pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 171.08362pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H^{2}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\partial}(M,T^{*}M),\mathrm{d}^{\nabla})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

(3.3)

where
\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{Aff}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}
denote respectively the sheaves of locally defined functions on
M
that are affine and integral affine with respect to
\Lambda
. The first map in this sequence is the connecting homomorphism induced by the short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{d}}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

while the last map is the isomorphism induced by the fine resolution

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{Aff}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\circ\mathrm{d}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{1}_{\partial}(-,T^{*}M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{2}_{\partial}(-,T^{*}M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}}
\textstyle{\dots}

(3.4)

Theorem 3.15
.

If
\theta
is a Lagrangian connection for a principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
, then (
3.3
) maps the Lagrangian Chern class
c_{1}(S,\omega)
to the class
[K_{\theta}]
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a good open cover of
M
such that
\mu
admits a Lagrangian section
\sigma_{i}
over
U_{i}
for each
i\in I
. The class
c_{1}(S,\omega)\in\check{H}^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})
is represented by the
1
-cocycle
\tau=[\tau_{ij}]\in\check{C}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})
, defined by the relation:

\tau_{ij}(x)\cdot\sigma_{i}(x)=\sigma_{j}(x),\quad x\in U_{ij}.

The connecting homomorphism in (
3.3
) sends
c_{1}(S,\omega)
to the class represented by the
2
-cocycle
\check{\mathrm{d}}f\in\check{C}^{2}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda})
, where
f=[f_{ij}]\in\check{C}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{C}^{\infty})
is any
1
-cochain lifting
\tau
(which exists since
U_{ij}
is contractible for all
i
and
j
). The last map in (
3.3
) is induced by the ‘Čech-de Rham’-type double complex

\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\check{C}^{2}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{C}^{\infty})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\check{C}^{2}(\mathcal{U},\Omega^{1}_{\partial}(-,T^{*}M))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\check{C}^{2}(\mathcal{U},\Omega^{2}_{\partial}(-,T^{*}M))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dots}
\textstyle{\check{C}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{C}^{\infty})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\check{C}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Omega^{1}_{\partial}(-,T^{*}M))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\check{C}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Omega^{2}_{\partial}(-,T^{*}M))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dots}
\textstyle{\check{C}^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{C}^{\infty})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\check{C}^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Omega^{1}_{\partial}(-,T^{*}M))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\check{C}^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Omega^{2}_{\partial}(-,T^{*}M))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dots}

In this complex the vertical differentials are those of the Čech complex and the horizontal differentials are induced by (
3.4
). Notice that both the rows and columns are exact at the
(p,q)^{\textrm{th}}
entry whenever
p,q>0
. We need to show that
(\check{\mathrm{d}}f,0,0)
and
(0,0,K_{\theta})
represent the same class in the cohomology of the total complex. This follows because

(\check{\mathrm{d}}f,0,0)-(0,0,K_{\theta})=\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{Tot}}(f,\sigma^{*}\theta),

where
\sigma^{*}\theta\in\check{C}^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Omega^{1}_{\partial}(M,T^{*}M))
is given by
(\sigma^{*}\theta)_{i}:=\sigma_{i}^{*}\theta
. Note that
\sigma_{i}^{*}\theta
belongs to the kernel of
\partial
because
\sigma_{i}
and
\theta
are Lagrangian and, by Lemma
3.12
, one has

\partial(\sigma_{i}^{*}\theta)=\sigma_{i}^{*}\omega_{\theta}=\sigma_{i}^{*}\omega=0.

∎

3.4.
Flat Lagrangian connections

Flat Lagrangian connections will play a key role in the study of invariant Kähler metrics. In this subsection we show how Lagrangian fibrations with such connections can be classified in terms of a sheaf cohomology group, much like principal
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-bundles with flat connection.

Definition 3.16
.

A
flat Lagrangian fibration
is a Lagrangian fibration equipped with a flat Lagrangian connection.

Note that, by Proposition
3.14
, a flat Lagrangian connection is the same thing as an invariant Lagrangian foliation transverse to the fibers.

Remark 3.17
.

In the literature on T-duality and mirror symmetry flat Lagrangian fibrations in our sense are sometimes called
semi-flat
(see, e.g.,
[
5
]
).

Given an integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
we denote by
\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}}
the sheaf of
\nabla
-flat sections of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
. If
(U,x^{i})
an integral affine chart then the flat sections of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{U}
take the form

s=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\mathrm{d}x^{i}\pmod{\Lambda},

where the coefficients
c_{i}
are locally constant. This shows that every flat section is automatically a Lagrangian section. Hence,
\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}}
is a subsheaf of
\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}}
and we have a map in cohomology

\check{H}^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}})\to\check{H}^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}}).

This map is part of an exact sequence, which will allow us to express the first obstruction.

Proposition 3.18
.

Given a symplectic torus bundle
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
the sequence

\textstyle{\check{H}^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\check{H}^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\partial}(M,T^{*}M),\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}),}

(3.5)

where the second map is given by (
3.3
), is exact.

Proof of Proposition
3.18
.

Consider the map of short exact sequences:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{d}}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{Aff}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}}/\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

By naturality of the connecting homomorphism, the induced square:

\textstyle{\check{H}^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\check{H}^{2}(M,\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\check{H}^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}}/\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\check{H}^{2}(M,\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{Aff}})}

commutes. So, up to isomorphism, (
3.5
) is part of the long exact sequence of:

0\to\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}}\to\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}}\to\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}}/\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}}\to 0.

∎

Combining this result, Proposition
3.14
and Theorem
3.15
we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 3.19
.

Fix an integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
. Given a Lagrangian fibration
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
inducing
\Lambda
the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
admits a flat Lagrangian connection;

(ii)

the Lagrangian-Chern class
c_{1}(S,\omega)\in\check{H}^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})
lies in the image of (
3.5
).

Flat Lagrangian fibrations are classified by their
flat Chern class
. This is the element in
\check{H}^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}})
constructed as the usual Chern class but using only flat local sections.
More precisely, two flat Lagrangian fibrations
\mu_{i}:(S_{i},\omega_{i},\theta_{i})\to(M,\Lambda)
are said to be
equivalent
if there exists an equivalence of Lagrangian fibrations preserving the connections:

\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 14.76515pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.59721pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.52776pt\raise 5.39168pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.39168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Psi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.59721pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-14.76515pt\raise-15.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mu_{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 32.59721pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{S_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.19443pt\raise-15.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mu_{2}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.19443pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-8.39583pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.94441pt\raise-36.76385pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\kern 32.7986pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}\qquad\Psi^{*}\theta_{2}=\theta_{1}.

Then one has the following classification result.

Theorem 3.20
.

Fix an integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
. Assigning to a flat Lagrangian fibration its flat Chern class yields a canonical 1:1 correspondence

\left\{\\
\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-11.94444pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces flat Lagrangian fibrations}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces$\mu:(S,\omega,\theta)\to M$}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces up to equivalence \,}\crcr}}}}\right\}\ \tilde{\longleftrightarrow}\ \check{H}^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}})}}}

Moreover, the flat Chern class is mapped to the Lagrangian Chern class by (
3.5
).

Proof.

Using flat sections, the proof of the theorem is entirely similar to the proof of Theorem
3.4
(see, e.g.,
[
19
]
or
[
14
]
).
∎

Remark 3.21
.

Note that
\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}}
is the locally constant sheaf associated to the representation of
\pi_{1}(M)
on
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
given by the holonomy of the integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
. In particular, if the holonomy representation if trivial then the cohomology of
\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}}
is that of
M
with values in the abelian group
\mathbb{T}^{m}
, where
m=\dim(M)
. For example, when
M
is simply-connected one obtains that the cohomology vanishes in degree one.

Finally, we observe that one can specialize the correspondence given in Proposition
3.5
to obtain the following useful description of flat Lagrangian fibrations over complete integral affine manifolds.

Corollary 3.22
.

Let
(M,\Lambda)
be a complete integral affine manifold (meaning that
M\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n}/\Gamma
) and let
H
be the canonical horizontal distribution of
\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
. There is a 1:1 correspondence

\left\{\\
\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-11.94444pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces flat Lagrangian fibrations}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces$\mu:(S,\omega,\theta)\to M$}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces up to equivalence \,}\crcr}}}}\right\}\quad\tilde{\longleftrightarrow}\quad\left\{\\
\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-16.27776pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces symplectic actions}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces$\Gamma\curvearrowright(\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\omega_{\mathrm{can}})$}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces covering $\Gamma\curvearrowright\mathbb{R}^{n}$}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces preserving $H$ \,}\crcr}}}}\right\}}}}}}}}

Example 3.23
.

A simple example illustrating the above correspondences can be obtained by considering the integral affine circle

(M,\Lambda)=(\mathbb{S}^{1},b\,\mathbb{Z}\,\mathrm{d}x)
, where
b>0
is a parameter. Then
H^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})=0
, so any Lagrangian fibration inducing
(M,\Lambda)
takes the form

\operatorname{pr}_{2}:(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{1},b\,\mathrm{d}y\wedge\mathrm{d}x)\to\mathbb{S}^{1}.

By the remark above, we have
H^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}})=\mathbb{S}^{1}
. A class
e^{2\pi ia}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
can be realized as the Lagrangian connection

D_{a}=\langle\partial_{x}-a\partial_{y}\rangle.

Example 3.24
.

Consider the second Lagrangian fibration

(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{2},\omega_{a})\to\mathbb{T}^{2}

in Example
3.7
. We saw there that for
a\in]0,1[
this fibration has non-trivial Lagrangian-Chern class. On the other hand, the canonical horizontal distribution of
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is invariant under the action (
3.1
), so it follows from Corollary
3.22
that this Lagrangian fibration is flat.

4.
Toric Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces a.k.a. toric Lagrangian fibrations

The main class of Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces that we study in this paper is a generalization of both principal Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces (i.e., Lagrangian fibrations) and classical symplectic toric manifolds. They are defined as follows.

Definition 4.1
.

A Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
is called
toric
if it satisfies the following conditions.

(i)

The
\mathcal{T}
-action is free on a dense subset of
S
.

(ii)

\dim(S)=2\dim(M)
.

(iii)

The map
\mu
has connected fibers and it is proper as map onto its image.
