6.83107pt}}_{{\kern-13.69072pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.83107pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}TS^{{\kern-7.09935pt{\mu}\kern 4.87932pt}}_{{\kern-11.73897pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.87932pt}}},T^{*}M).

It follows from Proposition
4.6
, the standard local normal form and the properties of a connection 1-form
\theta
that there is a unique
2
-form

K_{\theta}\in\Omega^{2}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-14.58315pt{b}\kern 12.36913pt}}_{{\kern-19.20953pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 12.36913pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-14.58315pt{b}\kern 12.36913pt}}_{{\kern-19.20953pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 12.36913pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-9.24052pt{b}\kern 7.62733pt}}_{{\kern-12.54509pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 7.62733pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-7.06128pt{b}\kern 5.44809pt}}_{{\kern-10.36584pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.44809pt}}},T^{*}\Delta)

such that

\mu^{*}K_{\theta}=\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\theta\in\Omega^{2}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}TS^{{\kern-14.15633pt{\mu}\kern 11.09273pt}}_{{\kern-20.6518pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 11.09273pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}TS^{{\kern-14.15633pt{\mu}\kern 11.09273pt}}_{{\kern-20.6518pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 11.09273pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}TS^{{\kern-9.0511pt{\mu}\kern 6.83107pt}}_{{\kern-13.69072pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.83107pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}TS^{{\kern-7.09935pt{\mu}\kern 4.87932pt}}_{{\kern-11.73897pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.87932pt}}},T^{*}M).

We call
K_{\theta}
the
curvature of the elliptic connection

\theta
.

Notice that
\nabla
also induces a flat
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-14.58315pt{b}\kern 12.36913pt}}_{{\kern-19.20953pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 12.36913pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-14.58315pt{b}\kern 12.36913pt}}_{{\kern-19.20953pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 12.36913pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-9.24052pt{b}\kern 7.62733pt}}_{{\kern-12.54509pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 7.62733pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-7.06128pt{b}\kern 5.44809pt}}_{{\kern-10.36584pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.44809pt}}}
-connection on
T^{*}\Delta
, with corresponding differential

\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}:\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-14.58315pt{b}\kern 12.36913pt}}_{{\kern-19.20953pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 7.62733pt}}_{{\kern-12.54509pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 7.62733pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-7.06128pt{b}\kern 5.44809pt}}_{{\kern-10.36584pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.44809pt}}},T^{*}\Delta)\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-14.58315pt{b}\kern 12.36913pt}}_{{\kern-19.20953pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 12.36913pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-14.58315pt{b}\kern 12.36913pt}}_{{\kern-19.20953pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 12.36913pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-9.24052pt{b}\kern 7.62733pt}}_{{\kern-12.54509pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 7.62733pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-7.06128pt{b}\kern 5.44809pt}}_{{\kern-10.36584pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.44809pt}}},T^{*}\Delta).

The curvature
K_{\theta}
is
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}
-closed and its cohomology class in
H^{2}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-14.58315pt{b}\kern 12.36913pt}}_{{\kern-19.20953pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 12.36913pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-14.58315pt{b}\kern 12.36913pt}}_{{\kern-19.20953pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 12.36913pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-9.24052pt{b}\kern 7.62733pt}}_{{\kern-12.54509pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 7.62733pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T\Delta^{{\kern-7.06128pt{b}\kern 5.44809pt}}_{{\kern-10.36584pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.44809pt}}},T^{*}\Delta)
does not depend on the choice of
\theta
. If
\theta
has zero radial residues, then by the proposition below the curvature is an honest smooth
2
-form
K_{\theta}\in\Omega^{2}(\Delta,T^{*}\Delta)
. So, in view of Proposition
4.29
, the connection-curvature construction actually defines a cohomology class
H^{2}(\Delta,T^{*}\Delta)
. In the case of torus actions this was also observed in
[
12
]
.

Proposition 4.30
.

Let
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}TS^{{\kern-14.15633pt{\mu}\kern 11.09273pt}}_{{\kern-20.6518pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 11.09273pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}TS^{{\kern-14.15633pt{\mu}\kern 11.09273pt}}_{{\kern-20.6518pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 11.09273pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}TS^{{\kern-9.0511pt{\mu}\kern 6.83107pt}}_{{\kern-13.69072pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.83107pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}TS^{{\kern-7.09935pt{\mu}\kern 4.87932pt}}_{{\kern-11.73897pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.87932pt}}},T^{*}M)
be an elliptic connection
1
-form for a toric Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
and suppose that
\theta
has zero radial residues. Then
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\theta\in\Omega^{2}(S,T^{*}M)
and
K_{\theta}\in\Omega^{2}(\Delta,T^{*}\Delta)
.
Moreover, if
\theta
is Lagrangian, then
K_{\theta}\in\Omega^{2}_{\partial}(\Delta,T^{*}\Delta)
, meaning that

\partial K_{\theta}(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}):=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{3}}(-1)^{|\sigma|}\langle K_{\theta}(v_{\sigma(1)},v_{\sigma(2)}),v_{\sigma(3)}\rangle=0.

Proof.

Note that
\iota_{\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)}(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\theta)=0
for all
1
-forms
\alpha
, since
\theta
is a connection
1
-form. Also
\iota_{\mathcal{E}_{S_{F}}}(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\theta)=0
for each open facet
F
, since
\theta
has zero radial residues. Hence,
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\theta
is a smooth
2
-form on
S
. Since
\mu_{*}(\mathcal{E}_{S_{F}})=2\,\mathcal{E}_{F}
, it follows that
K_{\theta}
is a smooth
2
-form as well. Finally,
\partial K_{\theta}=0
if
\theta
is Lagrangian, since this holds on
\mathring{\Delta}
by Proposition
3.14
, so it holds on all of
\Delta
by density of
\mathring{\Delta}
.
∎

This proposition shows that a choice of elliptic Lagrangian connection with zero radial residue gives rise to a cohomology class

[K_{\theta}]\in H^{2}(\Omega_{\partial}^{\bullet}(\Delta,T^{*}\Delta),\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}),

with the complex
(\Omega_{\partial}^{\bullet}(\Delta,T^{*}\Delta),\mathrm{d}^{\nabla})
defined like in Section
3.3
. In order to extend Theorem
3.15
, consider the composite

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 19.68755pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-19.68755pt\raise (4.6)

where
\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{Aff}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}
denote respectively the sheaves of locally defined functions on
\Delta
that are affine and integral affine with respect to
\Lambda
. The first map in this sequence is the connecting homomorphism induced by the short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{d}}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

while the last map is the isomorphism induced by the fine resolution

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{Aff}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\circ\mathrm{d}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{1}_{\partial}(-,T^{*}\Delta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{2}_{\partial}(-,T^{*}\Delta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}}
\textstyle{\dots}

Theorem 4.31
.

Let
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
be a toric Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space. If
\theta
is an elliptic Lagrangian connection for
\mu
with zero radial residue, then (
4.6
) maps the Lagrangian Chern class
c_{1}(S,\omega)
to the class
[K_{\theta}]
.

For the proof it will be useful to recall from
[
28
]
how, given a Delzant subspace
\Delta
of an integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
, one constructs the
canonical toric
\mathcal{T}
-space

\mu_{\Delta}:(S_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})\to M
with momentum map image
\Delta
and
c_{1}(S_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})=0
. This construction consists of the following steps:

(i)

As a topological space,
S_{\Delta}
is the quotient of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\Delta}
by the normal subgroupoid with isotropy group at
x\in\Delta
given by the subtorus with Lie algebra
(T_{x}F)^{0}
, the annihilator in
T_{x}^{*}M
of the tangent space to the open face
F
through
x
.

(ii)

The bundle projection of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
descends to a continuous map
\mu_{\Delta}:S_{\Delta}\to M
and the action of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
along its bundle projection descends to a continuous action of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
along
\mu_{\Delta}
.

(iii)

There are a unique smooth and symplectic structures on
S_{\Delta}
with the following property: for any integral affine chart
(U,\phi=(x^{1},...,x^{n}))
onto a connected open
\phi(U)
around the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
such that
\phi(U\cap\Delta)=\phi(U)\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
, the induced homeomorphism

\textstyle{\mu_{\Delta}^{-1}(U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mu_{k,n}^{-1}(\phi(U))}

\displaystyle\Big[\sum_{j=1}^{n}\theta_{j}\mathrm{d}x^{j}\Big]

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(e^{2\pi i\theta_{1}}\sqrt{x^{1}},\dots,e^{2\pi i\theta_{k}}\sqrt{x^{k}},\theta_{k+1},\dots,\theta_{n},x^{k+1},\dots,x^{n}\right)

is a symplectomorphism with respect to
\omega_{k,n}
.

All together, this gives a toric
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-space

\mu_{\Delta}:(S_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})\to M

with moment map image
\Delta
and trivial Lagrangian Chern class (by definition). Note that this comes with a canonical symplectic open embedding

\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\mathring{\Delta}}\hookrightarrow S_{\Delta}

onto the dense subset
\mathring{S}_{\Delta}=\mu_{\Delta}^{-1}(\mathring{\Delta})
.

Proof of Theorem
4.31
.

Consider
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
a good open cover of
\Delta
together with equivalences of Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces

\textstyle{(\mu^{-1}(U_{i}),\omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{(\mu_{\Delta}^{-1}(U_{i}),\omega_{\Delta})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\Delta}}
\textstyle{M}

For any two
i,j\in I
there is a unique Lagrangian section
\tau_{ij}\in\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}}(U_{ij})
such that

\tau_{ij}(\mu(p))\cdot\Psi_{j}(p)=\Psi_{i}(p),\quad p\in\mu^{-1}(U_{ij}).

The class
c_{1}(S,\omega)
is represented by the Čech
1
-cocycle
\tau=[\tau_{ij}]\in\check{C}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})
. The zero-section of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
induces a continuous section
\sigma_{\Delta}:\Delta\to S_{\Delta}
of
\mu_{\Delta}
, which is smooth and Lagrangian on
\mathring{\Delta}
and we set

\sigma_{i}:=\psi_{i}^{-1}\circ\sigma_{\Delta}:U_{i}\to S.

(4.9)

The fact that
c_{1}(S,\omega)
is mapped to
[K_{\theta}]
by (
4.6
) follows from an argument identical to that in the proof of Theorem
3.15
, where the Lagrangian sections
\sigma_{i}
are now given by (
4.9
). There is however one technical issue to overcome; now the sections
\sigma_{i}
are not smooth on all of
U_{i}
, only on
\mathring{U}_{i}=U_{i}\cap\mathring{\Delta}
, so
a priori
we can we can only consider

\sigma_{i}^{*}\theta\in\Omega_{\partial}^{1}(\mathring{U}_{i},T^{*}\Delta).

We need to show that the
1
-forms
\sigma_{i}^{*}\theta
extend smoothly to all of
U_{i}
for each
i\in I
. Since this is a local property, it can be checked in the standard toric coordinates for
\mu_{\Delta}
induced by integral affine coordinates
(U,x^{i})
as in (
iii
). In such toric coordinates
(\psi_{i}^{-1})^{*}\theta
is of the form

(\psi_{i}^{-1})^{*}\theta=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathrm{d}\phi_{j}\otimes\alpha_{j}+\mu_{k,n}^{*}(\mathrm{d}x^{j})\otimes\beta_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{n-k}\mathrm{d}\theta_{j}\otimes\alpha_{j+k}+\mu_{k,n}^{*}(\mathrm{d}x^{j+k})\otimes\beta_{j+k}

for
\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}\in\Omega^{1}(U_{i})
, because
\theta
has zero radial residues. Moreover,
\sigma_{\Delta}
is given by

\sigma_{\Delta}(x)=\left(\sqrt{x^{1}},\dots,\sqrt{x^{k}},0,\dots,0,x^{k+1},\dots,x^{n}\right).

Therefore,
\sigma_{i}^{*}\theta=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{d}x^{j}\otimes\beta_{j}
on
U\cap\mathring{U}_{i}
, so it extends smoothly to
U_{i}
. ∎

4.6.
Flat elliptic Lagrangian connections

Flat Lagrangian elliptic connections with zero radial residue will play a key role in the study of invariant Kähler metrics. In this subsection we extend the results of Section
3.4
to such connections.

Definition 4.32
.

A
flat toric Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
is a toric Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space equipped with a flat Lagrangian elliptic connection that has zero radial residues.

Given an integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
and a Delzant subspace
\Delta
, we denote by
\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}}
the sheaf on
\Delta
of
\nabla
-flat sections of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\Delta}
. This coincides with the restriction of the sheaf of flat sections of
\mathcal{T}
to
\Delta
and, as before, it is a subsheaf of
\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}}
. Also, the induced map in cohomology fits in an exact sequence

\textstyle{\check{H}^{1}(\Delta,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\check{H}^{1}(\Delta,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\partial}(\Delta,T^{*}\Delta),\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}),}

(4.10)

where the second map is given by (
4.6
). Therefore, Corollary
3.19
extends. The same goes for Theorem
3.20
.

Theorem 4.33
.

Fix a Delzant subspace
\Delta
of an integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
. There is a canonical 1:1 correspondence

\left\{\\
\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-11.94444pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces flat toric Hamiltonian $\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}$-spaces}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces$\mu:(S,\omega,\theta)\to M$}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces with $\mu(S)=\Delta$, up to equivalence \,}\crcr}}}}\right\}\ \tilde{\longleftrightarrow}\ \check{H}^{1}(\Delta,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}})}}}

The cohomology class corresponding to a flat toric Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space is mapped to its Lagrangian Chern class by the first map in (
4.10
).

For the proof of this, we use the following observation.

Proposition 4.34
.

The canonical toric
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu_{\Delta}:(S_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})\to M
admits a unique elliptic connection
\theta_{\Delta}
that restricts to the canonical flat Lagrangian connection on
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
via the canonical open embedding

\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\mathring{\Delta}}\hookrightarrow S_{\Delta}.

(4.11)

The connection
\theta_{\Delta}
is flat, Lagrangian and has zero radial residues.

Proof.

Uniqueness holds since the image of (
4.11
) is dense in
S_{\Delta}
. This also implies that for existence, it is enough to show that locally the canonical flat connection on
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\mathring{\Delta}}
extends to an elliptic connection
1
-form for
\mu_{\Delta}
with zero radial residues. So we can work in the toric coordinates induced by an integral affine chart
(U,x^{i})
, as in (
iii
), over which we also have an induced trivialization of the symplectic torus bundle
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\mathring{\Delta}}
. The embedding (
4.11
) becomes the map
\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathring{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{k}\to S_{k,n}
given by

\left(\theta_{1},...,\theta_{n},x^{1},...,x^{n}\right)\mapsto\left(e^{2\pi i\theta_{1}}\sqrt{x^{1}},\dots,e^{2\pi i\theta_{k}}\sqrt{x^{k}},\theta_{k+1},...,\theta_{n},x^{k+1},\dots,x^{n}\right).

On the other hand, the canonical flat connection on
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\mathring{\Delta}}
becomes
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{d}\theta_{i}\otimes\mathrm{d}x^{i}
so corresponds under (
4.11
) with the restriction to
\mathring{S}_{\Delta}
of the connection given in Example
4.28
. Therefore, the canonical flat connection on
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\mathring{\Delta}}
indeed extends to an elliptic connection
1
-form for
\mu_{\Delta}
with zero radial residues. Since the canonical connection on
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
is flat and Lagrangian and
\mathring{S}_{\Delta}
is dense in
S_{\Delta}
, it follows that
\theta_{\Delta}
is also flat and Lagrangian.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.33
.

The proof is virtually the same as that in the case of Lagrangian fibrations, where we now take the equivalence class of the flat toric Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-space of the above proposition as the equivalence class corresponding to the zero element of the group
\check{H}^{1}(\Delta,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}})
.
∎

Example 4.35
.

Consider the Delzant subspace
\Delta=\mathbb{S}^{1}\times[-1,1]
of the standard cylinder
(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R},\Lambda_{\mathrm{st}})
. Notice that

\check{H}^{1}(\Delta,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})=\check{H}^{2}(\Delta,\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda})=0,

so every toric
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-space is isomorphic to the space
\mu:(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2},\omega)\to\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}
of Example
2.20
.

Since
\Lambda
has trivial holonomy representation it follows – see Remark
3.21
– that the equivalence classes of flat
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-toric fibrations are in 1-1 correspondence with

\check{H}^{1}(\Delta,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}})=\check{H}^{1}(\Delta,\mathbb{T}^{2})=\mathbb{T}^{2}.

For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, the classes
(e^{2\pi ia},e^{2\pi ib})\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
can be realized by the elliptic connections on
\mu:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\to\Delta
given by the connection
1
-form

\theta_{a,b}=\left(-a\mathrm{d}x+\mathrm{d}y\right)\otimes\mathrm{d}x+\left(-b\mathrm{d}h+\mathrm{d}\phi\right)\otimes\mathrm{d}h.

This corresponds to the Lagrangian foliation on
\mu^{-1}(]-1,1[)
given by

D_{a,b}:=\left\langle\partial_{x}+a\partial_{y},\partial_{h}+b\partial_{\phi}\right\rangle.

Example 4.36
.

Consider the Delzant subspace
\Delta=\mathbb{S}^{1}\times[-1,1]
of the non-standard cylinder
(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times]-2,\infty[,\mathbb{Z}((h+2)\mathrm{d}x+x\mathrm{d}h)\oplus\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{d}h)
, as in Example
2.21
. We still have
\check{H}^{1}(\Delta,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})=0
so it follows that every toric
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-space is isomorphic to the space
\mu:(\mathbb{T}^{2}\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.95134pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\cr$\times$\cr}}}\mathbb{S}^{2},\omega)\to\mathbb{S}^{1}\times]-2,\infty[
appearing in Example
2.21
.

A computation using the Mayer-Vietoris sequence, shows that

\check{H}^{1}(\Delta,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}})=\check{H}^{1}(\Delta,\mathbb{T}^{2})=\mathbb{T}^{2},

so we conclude that equivalence classes of flat
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-toric fibrations are parameterized by
(e^{2\pi ia},e^{2\pi ib})\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
, with
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. These can be realized by the elliptic connections on
\mu:(\mathbb{T}^{2}\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.95134pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\cr$\times$\cr}}}\mathbb{S}^{2},\omega)\to\mathbb{S}^{1}\times]-2,\infty[
given by the connection
1
-form

\theta_{a,b}=(h+2)\left(-a\mathrm{d}x+\mathrm{d}y\right)\otimes\mathrm{d}x+\left(-b\mathrm{d}h+\mathrm{d}\phi+x\mathrm{d}y\right)\otimes\mathrm{d}h.

This corresponds to the Lagrangian foliation on
\mu^{-1}(]-1,1[)
given by

D_{a,b}:=\left\langle\partial_{x}+a(\partial_{y}-x\partial_{\phi}),\partial_{h}+b\partial_{\phi}\right\rangle.

5.
Delzant construction

The proof of the classification theorem of toric Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces (Theorem
4.12
) requires the construction of a toric Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
with moment map image
\mu(S)
a given Delzant subspace
\Delta\subset(M,\Lambda)
.

In the case of a Delzant polytope
\Delta\subset(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{Z}^{n})
Delzant gave a construction of a compact symplectic toric manifold with moment map image
\Delta
by symplectic reduction of a linear Hamiltonian torus action on
(\mathbb{C}^{d},\omega_{\mathrm{st}})
, where
d
is the number of facets. For a general Delzant subspace, as we recalled in Section
4.5
, the construction of canonical toric
\mathcal{T}
-space uses a different method. In this section we will give a generalization of Delzant’s construction for a large class of Delzant subspaces. Although this construction does not apply to all Delzant subspaces, it does cover many natural examples and has the advantage of realizing the desired toric space via symplectic reduction of a rather simple symplectic manifold.

5.1.
The primitive outward pointing normals

The aim of this section is to show for a Delzant subspace
\Delta
in a 1-connected integral affine manifold each facet of
\Delta
admits a naturally defining integral affine function.

In order to state a precise result, we recall that for a manifold with corners
\Delta
, given
x\in\partial\Delta
the cone
C_{x}(\Delta)
consists of all inward-pointing tangent vectors at
x
. We further denote by
\mathcal{S}^{1}(\Delta)
the collection of codimension
1
strata of
\Delta
(i.e., the open facets of
\Delta
).

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
(M,\Lambda)
be a
1
-connected integral affine manifold and let
\Delta
be a Delzant subspace. For each open facet
\Sigma
of
\Delta
, there is a unique integral affine function
\ell=\ell_{\Sigma}\in\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}(M)
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

\ell|_{\Sigma}=0
and
\ker(\mathrm{d}\ell)|_{\Sigma}=T\Sigma
;

(ii)

\ell
is outward-pointing, i.e.,
\mathrm{d}_{x}\ell(v)\leq 0
for all
v\in C_{x}(\Delta)
and all
x\in\Sigma
;

(iii)

for any
f\in\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}(M)
with property (i), there is a
k\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
f=k\,\ell
.

Moreover, any
x\in\Delta
admits an open neighbourhood
U
in
M
such that

\Delta\cap U=\bigcap_{\{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(\Delta)\colon x\in\overline{\Sigma}\}}\{\ell_{\Sigma}\leq 0\}\cap U.

(5.1)

The number of open facets of
\Delta
with
x
in their closure equals the depth of
x
in
\Delta
.

Given a Delzant subspace
\Delta
of a
1
-connected integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
we let

\mathcal{H}(\Delta):=\{\ell_{\Sigma}\mid\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(\Delta)\}

(5.2)

be the collection of integral affine functions
\ell_{\Sigma}
associated to the open facets of
\Delta
given by the previous proposition. We call the elements of
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)

primitive boundary defining (integral affine) functions
.

Example 5.2
.

It can happen that the same integral affine function is associated to different codimension
1
strata. An example of this is

M:=\mathbb{R}^{2}-\bigsqcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\{k\}\times[0,\infty[,\quad\Delta=M\cap(\mathbb{R}\times]-\infty,0]),

where
M
is equipped with the integral affine structure inherited from
(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{Z}^{2})
. Then
\Delta
in fact has infinitely many codimension
1
strata, but
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
consist of a single function.

For the proof of Proposition
5.1
, we first recall the following fact.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
(V^{*},\Lambda)
be an integral affine vector space. Suppose that
H\subset V
is an hyperplane, which is primitive in the sense that
\operatorname{span}{R}(H\cap\Lambda^{\vee})=H
. Then there is an
\alpha\in\Lambda
such that:

(i)

H=\ker(\alpha)
;

(ii)

if
\beta\in\Lambda
and
H=\ker(\beta)
, then
\beta=k\,\alpha
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Moreover,
\alpha
is unique up to a sign.

We call
\alpha\in\Lambda
, as in this lemma, a
primitive normal covector
to the hyperplane
H
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
(M,\Lambda)
be an integral affine manifold, let
\Delta
be a Delzant subspace and let
\Sigma
be a codimension
1
stratum of
\Delta
. For each
x\in\Sigma
, there is a unique outward-pointing primitive normal covector
\alpha_{x}\in\Lambda_{x}
to the hyperplane
T_{x}\Sigma\subset(T_{x}M,\Lambda^{\vee}_{x})
. The family of all such outward-pointing primitive normals is holonomy-invariant.

In this lemma, by ’holonomy invariant’ we mean that for any path
\gamma
in
\Sigma
, the holonomy representation
\operatorname{hol}([\gamma]):T_{\gamma(0)}M\to T_{\gamma(1)}M
pulls
\alpha_{\gamma(1)}
back to
\alpha_{\gamma(0)}
.

Proof.

The existence and uniqueness of the covectors
\alpha_{x}
follows from the previous lemma and the fact that open facets of Delzant subspaces are primitive codimension
1
submanifolds of
(M,\Lambda)
. By the uniqueness of primitive normal covectors, to prove holonomy-invariance it suffices to show that for any path
\gamma
in
\Sigma
, the holonomy representation
\operatorname{hol}([\gamma]):T_{\gamma(0)}M\to T_{\gamma(1)}M
maps the tangent cone
C_{\gamma(0)}(\Delta)
onto the tangent cone
C_{\gamma(1)}(\Delta)
. It is enough to check this for a path contained in the domain of an integral affine chart.

Let
(U,\phi=(x^{1},\dots,x^{m}))
be an integral affine chart adapted to
\Delta
in the sense that
\phi(U\cap\Delta)
is an open in
[0,\infty[\times\mathbb{R}^{m-1}
around the origin. Then for any point
x\in\phi^{-1}(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{m-1})
we have

C_{x}(\Delta)=\textrm{Cone}\left(\partial_{x^{1}},\pm\partial_{x^{2}}\dots,\pm\partial_{x^{m}}\right).

If
\gamma
is a path lying in an open facet
\Sigma\cap U\subset\phi^{-1}(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{m-1})
, then
\operatorname{hol}([\gamma])
maps
C_{\gamma(0)}(\Delta)
onto
C_{\gamma(1)}(\Delta)
, since the frames induced by integral affine coordinate charts are flat and the coordinate vector fields
\partial_{x^{i}}
are invariant under the action of
\operatorname{hol}([\gamma])
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Let
\Sigma
be a codimension
1
stratum of
\Delta
. Since
M
is
1
-connected, for any
x_{0}\in\Sigma
there is a unique parallel
1
-form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(M)
that evaluates at
x_{0}
to the the outward-pointing primitive normal covector. By the holonomy-invariance in Lemma
5.4
, for any
x\in\Sigma
the value
\alpha_{x}
is the outward-pointing primitive normal covector at
x
. As
M
is
1
-connected, there is a unique function
\ell=\ell_{\Sigma}\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that
\mathrm{d}\ell=\alpha
and
\ell|_{\Sigma}=0
. Since
\alpha
is parallel and
\alpha_{x_{0}}\in\Lambda_{x_{0}}
,
\alpha
takes values in
\Lambda
. Hence,
\ell
is an integral affine function. It satisfies the required properties (i)–(iii) and is the unique such integral affine function, due to the properties of the covectors in Lemma
5.4
and the fact that the differential of an integral affine function is a parallel
1
-form.

Next, fix
x\in\Delta
of depth
k
. Pick an integral affine chart
(U,\phi=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n}))
centered at
x
such that

W:=\phi(U\cap\Delta)
is a convex open set in
[0,\infty[^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-k}
. The set of points in
U\cap\Delta
of depth
1
corresponds to the disjoint union of the convex subsets

W_{i}:=\left\{(x^{1},\dots,x^{m})\in W\mid x^{i}=0\textrm{ and }x^{j}>0\textrm{ for }1\leq j\leq k,\text{ }j\neq i\right\},

for
i=1,\dots,k
. Since each
W_{i}
is connected,
\phi^{-1}(W_{i})
is contained in a single open facet
\Sigma_{i}
of
\Delta
, with defining function satisfying
\ell_{\Sigma_{i}}|_{U}=-x^{i}
. The latter shows that
\Sigma_{i}\neq\Sigma_{j}
if
i\neq j
. Therefore, the number of open facets with
x
in their closure is indeed equal to
k
. It is now also clear that (
5.1
) holds for this choice of
U
.
∎

5.2.
Delzant subspaces of finite type

Let
(M,\Lambda)
be a connected integral affine manifold and let
\Delta
be a connected Delzant subspace. Consider the canonical map

(i_{\Delta})_{*}:\widetilde{\Delta}\to\widetilde{M},

from the universal cover of
\Delta
into that of
M
, relative to some choice of base-point. The image
\widehat{\Delta}:=(i_{\Delta})_{*}(\widetilde{\Delta})
is a Delzant subspace of
\widetilde{M}
. For the Delzant subspaces to which our construction applies, the collection
\mathcal{H}(\widehat{\Delta})
of primitive boundary defining functions is finite and determines the Delzant subspace in the following sense.

Definition 5.5
.

A connected Delzant subspace
\Delta
is of
finite type
if
\mathcal{H}(\widehat{\Delta})
is finite and

\widehat{\Delta}=\bigcap_{f\in\mathcal{H}(\widehat{\Delta})}\{f\leq 0\}.

(5.3)

Below we give two examples illustrating how each of these conditions may fail.

Example 5.6
.

Let

M:=\mathbb{R}^{2}-\{(0,y)\mid y\geq 0\}

with the standard integral affine structure
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{d}x\oplus\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{d}y
. Consider the Delzant subspace

\Delta=\left\{(x,y)\in M\mid y\leq|x|\right\}.

This is closed in
M
and has two open facets, with primitive boundary defining functions
\ell_{\pm}(x,y)=\pm(x-y)
. However,
\Delta
does not satisfy (
5.3
).

Example 5.7
.

Consider the non-standard integral affine cylinder

(M,\Lambda):=(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{d}x\oplus\mathbb{Z}(\mathrm{d}h-x\mathrm{d}x))

and the Delzant subspace

\Delta=\left\{(x,h)\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}\mid h\leq\tfrac{1}{2}x^{2}-\tfrac{1}{8},\ -\tfrac{1}{2}\leq x\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\right\}.

The lift
\widehat{\Delta}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}=\widetilde{M}
is the region bounded from above by the curves

\left\{h=\tfrac{1}{2}(x+n)^{2}-\tfrac{1}{8}\right\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}.

In this example, even though
\Delta
has only one open facet, the collection
\mathcal{H}(\widehat{\Delta})
is infinite, as it consists of the functions

\ell_{n}(x,h)=h-\tfrac{1}{2}(x+n)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{8},\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}.

In both of these examples the Delzant subspace is not compact. We do not know of an example of a compact Delzant subspace that is not of finite type.

In the coming subsection, the following property of Delzant subspaces of finite type will be important.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
(M,\Lambda)
be a
1
-connected integral affine manifold,
\Delta
a Delzant subspace of finite type and
x\in\Delta
. Consider the primitive boundary defining functions
\ell\in\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
for which
\ell(x)=0
.

(i)

The number of such functions equals the depth of
x
.

(ii)

Their differentials at
x
can be extended to a basis of
\Lambda_{x}
and span
(T_{x}F)^{0}
.

Proof of Proposition
5.8
.

Let
x\in\Delta
. By Proposition
5.1
the number of
\ell\in\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
for which
\ell(x)=0
is at least
k:=0pt_{\Delta}(x)
. To show that these are in fact equal, given
\ell_{0}\in\mathcal{H}(\Delta)
such that
\ell_{0}(x)=0
, we need to prove that
\ell_{0}
is one of the functions
\ell_{i}
associated to one of the
k
open facets
\Sigma_{i}
with
x
in their closure. Choose an integral affine chart
(U,\phi=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n}))
centered at
x
, as in the second part of the proof of Proposition
5.1
. Since
\ell_{0}
is affine and vanishes at
x
, its restriction to
U
is of the form:

\ell_{0}|_{U}=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}t_{i}x^{i},\quad t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}.

In view of (
5.3
),
\ell_{0}
is non-positive on
\Delta
and so
t_{i}\geq 0
for all
i=1,\dots,k
and
t_{i}=0
for all
i=k+1,\dots,n
.
As in the proof of Proposition
5.1
,
\ell_{i}|_{U}=-x^{i}
for each
i=1,..,k
. So,

\ell_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}t_{i}\ell_{i},\quad t_{i}\geq 0,

since this equality holds on
U
and the functions on both sides are affine. It follows from property (iii) in Proposition
5.1
that, if
\ell_{0}\neq\ell_{i}
for all
i=1,\dots,k
, then there are at least two distinct
i_{1},i_{2}\in\{1,\dots,k\}
for which
t_{i_{1}},t_{i_{2}}\neq 0
. In that case, since
\Delta
satisfies (
5.3
),
\Sigma_{0}
must be contained in the codimension
2
submanifold
\{\ell_{i_{1}}=\ell_{i_{2}}=0\}
, which is a contradiction. So, our first claim follows. Moreover, the fact that
\ell_{i}|_{U}=-x^{i}
for all
i=1,...,k
shows that the differentials of these functions at
x
extend to a basis of
\Lambda_{x}
and span
(T_{x}F)^{0}
.
∎

5.3.
The 1-connected case

In this section we give a Delzant type construction for a finite type Delzant subspace of a
1
-connected integral affine manifold. Treating the 1-connected case first is convenient for the proof of the general case, and moreover it is interesting on its own, as it already covers a class of non-compact toric symplectic manifolds (cf. Section
4.3
).

Let
(M,\Lambda)
be a 1-connected integral affine manifold and let
\Delta
be a Delzant subspace of finite type so

\Delta=\bigcap_{i=1}^{d}\{\ell_{i}\leq 0\},

where
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{d}
are the primitive boundary defining functions. We consider the map:

\phi:=(\ell_{1},...,\ell_{d}):(M,\Lambda)\to(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{Z}^{d}).

Since this map is integral affine, it induces a morphism of symplectic torus bundles

(\phi,\underline{\phi}^{*}):(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})\dashrightarrow(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathrm{d}\theta_{i}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{i})

as in Proposition
2.16
. This, in turn, induces a Hamiltonian
\mathbb{T}^{d}
-action on
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
with moment map the composition of
\phi
with the bundle projection
\pi
and action given by

(\theta_{1},...,\theta_{d})\cdot[\alpha]=\Big[\alpha+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\theta_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{i}\Big].

Theorem 5.9
.

Consider the diagonal Hamiltonian
\mathbb{T}^{d}
-action with moment map

\mu_{\phi}:=\phi+(|z_{1}|^{2},...,|z_{d}|^{2}):(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})\times(\mathbb{C}^{d},\omega_{\textrm{st}})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}.

The
\mathbb{T}^{d}
-action is free on
\mu_{\phi}^{-1}(0)
and the symplectic quotient

((\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}\times\mathbb{C}^{d})\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathbb{T}^{d},\omega_{\mathrm{red}})

is a toric Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-space for the action induced by translations on the first factor. Moreover, this space is equivalent to the canonical toric Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-space
\mu_{\Delta}:(S_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})\to M
.

Proof.

We start by checking the diagonal
\mathbb{T}^{d}
-action on
\mu_{\phi}^{-1}(0)
is free, so the symplectic quotient is well-defined. Let
([\alpha_{x}],z)\in\mu_{\phi}^{-1}(0)
and
\theta=(\theta_{1},...,\theta_{d})\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
be such that
\theta
fixes
([\alpha_{x}],z)
. Since
\theta
fixes
z
, it follows that
\theta_{i}=0\mod\mathbb{Z}
whenever
z_{i}\neq 0
. On the other hand, because
\ell_{i}(x)=-|z_{i}|^{2}
, the remaining
i\in\{1,...,d\}
, say
i_{1},...,i_{k}
, are those for which
\ell_{i}(x)=0
. So, by Proposition
5.8
,
(\mathrm{d}\ell_{i_{1}})_{x},\dots,(\mathrm{d}\ell_{i_{k}})_{x}
extends to a basis of
\Lambda_{x}
. Hence, the fact that
\theta
fixes
[\alpha_{x}]
implies that
\theta_{i_{1}}=...=\theta_{i_{k}}=0\mod\mathbb{Z}
as well.

Next, observe that the action of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
on itself by translation descends to a Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-action along

\mu:\big((\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}\times\mathbb{C}^{d})\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathbb{T}^{d},\omega_{\mathrm{red}}\big)\to M,\quad[\alpha,z]\mapsto\pi(\alpha),

which has moment map image

\bigcap_{i=1}^{d}\{\ell_{i}\leq 0\}=\Delta.

Finally, we claim that we have an equivalence of Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-spaces

\textstyle{(S_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{((\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}\times\mathbb{C}^{d})\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathbb{T}^{d},\omega_{\mathrm{red}}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{M}

given by

[\alpha_{x}]\longmapsto[\alpha_{x},(\sqrt{-\ell_{1}(x)},\dots,\sqrt{-\ell_{d}(x)})].

This map is a
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-equivariant bijection with inverse induced by

(\alpha_{x},z)\longmapsto[t^{-1}\cdot\alpha_{x}],

where
t=(t_{1},\dots,t_{d})
is given by

t_{i}=t_{i}(z,x):=\begin{cases}\tfrac{z_{i}}{|z_{i}|},\quad\text{if $\ell_{i}(x)\neq 0$},\\
\\
\ 0,\quad\text{if $\ell_{i}(x)=0$}.\end{cases}

To see that
\Psi
is a smooth map, we note that around a point
x_{0}
of depth
k
its expression in the chart (
iii
) is

(z_{1},\dots,z_{k},\theta_{k+1},\dots,\theta_{n},x^{k+1},\dots,x^{n})\mapsto\big[\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\theta_{i}\mathrm{d}x^{i},(w_{1},\dots,w_{d})\big],

where

w_{i}:=\begin{cases}\quad z_{i},\quad\text{if $\ell_{i}(x_{0})=0$},\\
\\
\sqrt{-\ell_{i}(x)},\quad\text{if $\ell_{i}(x_{0})\neq 0$}.\end{cases}

Finally, we observe that this map is a symplectomorphism. Indeed, over
\mathring{\Delta}
, where
k=0
, it is given by the symplectic map

(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n},x^{1},\dots,x^{n})\mapsto\big[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta_{i}\mathrm{d}x^{i},(\sqrt{-\ell_{1}(x)},\dots,\sqrt{-\ell_{d}(x)})\big].

By density, we conclude that
\Phi
is a symplectic map. Since it is a bijection between manifolds of the same dimension, it is a symplectomorphism.
∎

Example 5.10

(The standard Delzant construction)
.

One can recover the usual Delzant construction of a compact symplectic manifold from the construction in Theorem
5.9
via reduction by stages as follows.

Fix a Delzant polytope
\Delta\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
d
facets. In this case, Theorem
5.9
gives a symplectic toric manifold

\mu:\big((\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{d})\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathbb{T}^{d},\omega_{\mathrm{red}}\big)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(5.4)

If
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{d}
are the primitive boundary defining functions, one has a linear map

\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad e_{a}\mapsto\mathrm{d}\ell_{a},

where
\{e_{a}\}
is the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The kernel of this map is a Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which integrates to a subtorus
N\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Choosing a subtorus
\mathbb{T}^{n}\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}
complementary to
N
, one can first perform symplectic reduction by this subtorus. The resulting reduced space is canonically isomorphic to the
d
-dimensional complex vector space with its canonical symplectic form via the map

(\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{d})\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathbb{T}^{d},\omega_{\mathrm{red}})\to(\mathbb{C}^{d},\omega_{\mathrm{st}}),\quad(x,\theta,z)\mapsto(-\theta)\cdot z.

Therefore, the symplectic toric manifold (
5.4
) is canonically isomorphic to

\mu:(\mathbb{C}^{d}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}N,\omega_{\mathrm{red}})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(5.5)

This is the compact toric symplectic manifold constructed by Delzant
[
15
]
.

5.4.
The general construction

Let
(M,\Lambda)
be a connected integral affine manifold and let
\Delta
be a Delzant subspace of finite type. Consider the canonical map

(i_{\Delta})_{*}:\widetilde{\Delta}\to\widetilde{M},

from the universal cover of
\Delta
into that of
M
, relative to a fixed base-point in
\Delta
. The image
\widehat{\Delta}:=(i_{\Delta})_{*}(\widetilde{\Delta})
is a Delzant subspace of
\widetilde{M}
. Let
\Gamma
be the image of the group homomorphism
(i_{\Delta})_{*}:\pi_{1}(\Delta)\to\pi_{1}(M)
. Note that the canonical
\Gamma
-action on
\widetilde{M}
is by integral affine isomorphisms and preserves
\widehat{\Delta}
. Therefore, it induces an action on the set of primitive boundary defining functions
\mathcal{H}(\widehat{\Delta})
. Fixing an ordering
\mathcal{H}(\Delta)=\{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{d}\}
, this induces a homomorphism
\Gamma\to\text{Sym}(1,\dots,d)
and, therefore, actions on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
and
\mathbb{T}^{d}
by coordinate permutations. The group
\Gamma
also acts on
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}
via the cotangent lift of the action on
\widetilde{M}
. All together, we obtain a Hamiltonian action of the semi-direct product
\Gamma\ltimes\mathbb{T}^{d}
, with moment map

\mu_{\phi}:=\phi+(|z_{1}|^{2},...,|z_{d}|^{2}):(\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}},\Omega_{\widetilde{\Lambda}})\times(\mathbb{C}^{d},\omega_{\textrm{st}})\to\mathbb{R}^{d},

where
(\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}},\Omega_{\widetilde{\Lambda}})
is the symplectic torus bundle over
(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{\Lambda})
.

Theorem 5.11
.

The
\Gamma\ltimes\mathbb{T}^{d}
-action is free on
\mu_{\phi}^{-1}(0)
and the symplectic quotient

((\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}\times\mathbb{C}^{d})\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}(\Gamma\ltimes\mathbb{T}^{d}),\,\omega_{\mathrm{red}})

is a toric Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-space, which is equivalent to the canonical toric Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-space
\mu_{\Delta}:(S_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})\to M
.

Proof.

The fact that the
\Gamma\ltimes\mathbb{T}^{d}
-action on
\mu^{-1}_{\phi}(0)
is free and proper, follows using that the
\mathbb{T}^{d}
-action on this set is free and the
\Gamma
-action on
\widetilde{M}
is free and proper.

Next, note that the symplectic torus bundle
(\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}},\Omega_{\widetilde{\Lambda}})
is canonical isomorphic to the pull-back of
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
along the covering map
\widetilde{M}\to M
. The action of
(\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}},\Omega_{\widetilde{\Lambda}})
on the first factor in
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}\times\mathbb{C}^{d}
descends to a Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-action on the symplectic quotient. This makes it a toric
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-space.

For the last statement, we use the following lemma.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
(M,\Lambda)
be a connected integral affine manifold, let
\Delta
be a connected Delzant subspace and consider
\widehat{\Delta}
and
\Gamma
as above. The
\Gamma
-action on
\widehat{\Delta}
lifts to a symplectic action on
(S_{\widehat{\Delta}},\omega_{\widehat{\Delta}})
and there is an equivalence of Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-spaces

\textstyle{(S_{\widehat{\Delta}},\omega_{\widehat{\Delta}})/\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\underline{\mu_{\widetilde{\Delta}}}}
\textstyle{(S_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\Delta}}
\textstyle{M}

where
\underline{\mu_{\widetilde{\Delta}}}
is the composition of the map
S_{\widehat{\Delta}}/\Gamma\to\widetilde{M}/\Gamma
induced by
\mu_{\widetilde{\Delta}}
with the quotient map
\widetilde{M}/\Gamma\to M
.

Proof.

This readily follows from the naturality of the construction of the canonical toric space associated to a Delzant subspace (see
[
28
]
).
∎

Since
\mathbb{T}^{d}
is a normal subgroup of
\Gamma\ltimes\mathbb{T}^{d}
, there is an induced
\Gamma
-action on the symplectic quotient
((\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}\times\mathbb{C}^{d})\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\omega_{\mathrm{red}})
. The equivalence between
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}
-spaces

(S_{\widehat{\Delta}},\omega_{\widehat{\Delta}})\to(\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}\times\mathbb{C}^{d})\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\omega_{\mathrm{red}})

given by Theorem
5.9
is
\Gamma
-equivariant. Passing to the quotients, the equivalence in the statement follows from the previous lemma.
∎

Remark 5.13

(Realizing non-trivial Lagrangian-Chern classes)
.

Theorem
5.11
gives a Delzant type construction for toric
\mathcal{T}
-spaces with trivial Lagrangian-Chern class. This construction can be extended to the case of non-trivial Lagrangian-Chern class. After possible shrinking
M
, one can assume that any class in
\check{H}^{1}(\Delta,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})
can be realized as the restriction of the Lagrangian-Chern class of a principal
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-space
\mu_{P}:(P,\omega_{P})\to M
. Then a toric
\mathcal{T}
-space with a non-trivial class, can be realized as a symplectic quotient of the form

((q^{*}P\times\mathbb{C}^{d})\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}(\Gamma\ltimes\mathbb{T}^{d}),\,\omega_{\mathrm{red}})

where
q:\widetilde{M}\to M
is the covering map.

6.
Invariant Kähler metrics I: Lagrangian fibrations

In this section we study
\mathcal{T}
-invariant Kähler structures on
principal
Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces, i.e., Lagrangian fibrations. In the next section we will consider toric Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-spaces.

Definition 6.1
.

Given a Hamiltonian
\mathcal{T}
-space
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
, a
\mathcal{T}
-invariant Kähler structure
is a
\mathcal{T}
-invariant Kähler metric
G
on
S
with Kähler form
\omega
.

By Proposition
2.28
, the
\mathcal{T}
-invariance of the Kähler structure can be expressed as

\mathscr{L}_{\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)}G=0\quad\text{ if }\quad\nabla\alpha=0.

(6.1)

Since
\omega
is
\mathcal{T}
-invariant, this is equivalent to the
\mathcal{T}
-invariance of the complex structure
J:TS\to TS
, i.e.,

\mathscr{L}_{\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)}J=0\quad\text{ if }\quad\nabla\alpha=0.

(6.2)

Given a Lagrangian fibration
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
we will call a compatible Kähler metric
g
on
S

invariant
if it is
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-invariant, where
\Lambda\subset T^{*}M
is the induced integral affine structure on the base. Invariant Kähler metrics on a Lagrangian fibration are intimately related to Hessian metrics on the base of the fibration. We recall that a
Hessian metric
on an (integral) affine manifold
M
is a Riemannian metric
g
satisfying

\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}g^{\flat}=0.

(6.3)

where
g^{\flat}:TM\to T^{*}M
denotes the
T^{*}M
-valued form on
M
given by contraction with
g
. This condition is equivalent to the existence of a potential for the metric in each (integral) affine chart
(U,x^{i})
with contractible domain, i.e., a function
\phi\in C^{\infty}(U)
such that

g|_{U}=\sum_{i,j}\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}}\mathrm{d}x^{i}\otimes\mathrm{d}x^{j}.

We refer to
[
33
]
for details on Hessian metrics.

The main theorem of this section is the following.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
be a a Lagrangian fibration. A choice of invariant Kähler metric
G
on
S
makes
\mu:S\to M
into a Riemannian submersion, with the following properties:

(i)

(\ker\mathrm{d}\mu)^{\perp}
is a flat Lagrangian connection;

(ii)

The metric
g
induced on the base
M
is a Hessian metric.

Conversely, given a flat Lagrangian connection
D
and a Hessian metric
g
on
M
, there is unique invariant Kähler metric
G
on
S
inducing
g
such that
D=(\ker\mathrm{d}\mu)^{\perp}
.

Remark 6.3
.

This theorem shows that the existence problem for invariant Kähler metric on a Lagrangrian fibration reduces to two independent obstruction problems. As discussed in Section
3.4
, the obstruction to the existence of a flat Lagrangian connection for a Lagrangian fibration can be expressed in terms of its Lagrangian Chern class. The obstruction to the existence of a Hessian metric on an (integral) affine manifold are more subtle and have been studied by several authors (see, e.g.,
[
33
]
and the references therein).

Example 6.4
.

Consider the symplectic torus bundle in Example
2.8
. Its base
(\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{d}x^{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}(\mathrm{d}x^{2}-x^{1}\mathrm{d}x^{1}))
is an integral affine manifold which does not admit a Hessian metric. Indeed, this being a symplectic torus bundle, it admits a flat Lagrangian connection. However, its total space
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
does not admit any Kähler structure, as it is the Kodaira-Thurston symplectic manifold.

Notice that the symplectic manifold
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
admits another Lagrangian fibration

\mu:\mathcal{T}\to\mathbb{T}^{2},\quad[\theta_{1},\theta_{2},x^{1},x^{2}]\mapsto[\theta_{2},x^{1}].

The induced integral affine structure on the base
\mathbb{T}^{2}
is now the standard one. This fibration does not admit a Lagrangian section. In fact, due to Theorem
6.2
, it does not even admit a flat Lagrangian connection, since its base
(\mathbb{T}^{2},\Lambda_{\mathrm{st}})
admits a Hessian metric, but its total space is not Kähler.

Two equivalent Kähler Lagrangian fibrations induce the same metric on their base. Moreover, given two Kähler Lagrangian fibrations that induce the same Hessian metric on
M
, an equivalence of the underlying Lagrangian fibrations intertwines the Kähler structures if and only if it intertwines the orthogonal complements to the fibers. Therefore, we conclude the following from Theorems
3.20
and
6.2
.

Corollary 6.5
.

Given an integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
, there is a canonical 1:1 correspondence

\left\{\\
\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-12.0pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces K\"{a}hler Lagrangian fibrations}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces$\mu:(S,\omega,G)\to M$ }\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces up to equivalence \,}\crcr}}}}\right\}\ \tilde{\longleftrightarrow}\ \left\{\\
\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-5.8611pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces Hessian metrics }\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces$g$ on $M$\,}\crcr}}}}\right\}\ \times\check{H}^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}})}}}}}

Choosing an integral affine chart
(U,x^{i})
with contractible domain, there exist action-angle coordinates
(\mu^{-1}(U),x^{i},\theta_{j})
(see Remark
3.3
) in which the connection takes the form

\theta=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{d}\theta_{i}\otimes\mathrm{d}x^{i}.

In such coordinates the invariant Kähler metric
G
can be written as

G=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}g_{ij}\,\mathrm{d}x^{i}\otimes\mathrm{d}x^{j}+\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}g^{ij}\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{i}\otimes\mathrm{d}\theta_{j},

(6.4)

where

g_{ij}=\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}},

for some function
\phi\in C^{\infty}(U)
, and
(g^{ij})
denotes the inverse matrix of
(g_{ij})
. The corresponding complex structure takes the form

J=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}g_{ij}\,\mathrm{d}x^{i}\otimes\partial_{\theta_{j}}-\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}g^{ij}\mathrm{d}\theta_{i}\otimes\partial_{x^{j}}.

6.1.
Proof of Theorem
6.2

Let
\Lambda
be the induced integral affine structure on the base of
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
. Denote by
G
a
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-invariant Kähler metric on
S
. The distribution orthogonal to the fibers

D:=(\ker\mathrm{d}\mu)^{\perp}

is
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-invariant and the restriction of
G
to
D
is also
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-invariant. It follows that there is a unique Riemannian metric
g
on
M
such that
\mathrm{d}_{x}\mu:D_{x}\to T_{p}M
is an isometry for all
x\in\mu^{-1}(p)
, i.e.,
\mu:(S,G)\to(M,g)
is a Riemannian submersion.

Now observe that we can express
D
has:

D=J(\ker\mathrm{d}\mu),

where
J:TS\to TS
denotes the complex structure. Since
\ker\mathrm{d}\mu
is Lagrangian, we conclude that
D
is Lagrangian. It follows also that the vector fields
J\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)
, with
\alpha
flat 1-forms, generate
D
. Now,
J
is
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-invariant, so by (
6.2
) we find that:

[\mathbscr{a}(\alpha),J(\mathbscr{a}(\beta))]=(\mathscr{L}_{\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)}J)+J([\mathbscr{a}(\alpha),\mathbscr{a}(\beta)])=0,

for any flat 1-forms
\alpha
and
\beta
. Then the vanishing of the Nijenhuis torsion of
J
gives:

[J(\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)),J(\mathbscr{a}(\beta))]=J([J(\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)),\mathbscr{a}(\beta)]+[\mathbscr{a}(\alpha),J(\mathbscr{a}(\beta))])+[\mathbscr{a}(\alpha),\mathbscr{a}(\beta)]=0.

This shows that
D
is flat, so (i) holds.

To prove that
g
is an Hessian metric we will check that (
6.3
) holds:

\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}g^{\flat}(X,Y)=\nabla_{X}g^{\flat}(Y)-\nabla_{Y}g^{\flat}(X)-g^{\flat}([X,Y])=0,\quad(X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)).

To prove this it is enough to consider vector fields of the form
X=g^{\sharp}(\alpha)
and
Y=g^{\sharp}(\beta)
, with
\alpha
and
\beta
flat, since these locally generate all vector fields. Therefore, we are left to check that:

[g^{\sharp}(\alpha),g^{\sharp}(\beta)]=0\quad\text{ if }\quad\nabla\alpha=\nabla\beta=0.

Now observe that the definition of the metric
h
yields:

\mu_{*}(J(\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)))=g^{\sharp}(\alpha).

Hence, if
\alpha
and
\beta
are flat, this last relation and the computation above shows that:

[g^{\sharp}(\alpha),g^{\sharp}(\beta)]=\mu_{*}([J(\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)),J(\mathbscr{a}(\beta))]=0.

This shows that (ii) holds.

To prove the converse, note that given a flat Lagrangian connection
D
we can define a unique Riemannian metric
G
on
S
by requiring the following properties to hold:

(a)

\ker\mathrm{d}\mu
and
D
are orthogonal;

(b)

On
D
the metric is the unique one that gives isometries

\mathrm{d}_{p}\mu:(D_{p},G|_{D_{p}})\to(T_{\mu(p)}M,g_{\mu(p)});

(c)

On
\ker\mathrm{d}\mu
the metric is the unique one that gives isometries

(T^{*}_{\mu(p)}M,g^{-1}_{\mu(p)})\to(\ker\mathrm{d}_{p}\mu,G|_{\ker\mathrm{d}_{p}\mu}),\quad\alpha\mapsto\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)|_{p}.

The resulting Riemannian metric is clearly invariant and we are left to prove that it is a Kähler metric with Kähler form
\omega
.

Given
p\in S
and
\alpha\in T_{\mu(p)}M
there is a unique horizontal vector
\widetilde{g^{\sharp}(\alpha)}\in D_{p}
such that

\mathrm{d}_{p}\mu(\widetilde{g^{\sharp}(\alpha)})=g^{\sharp}(\alpha).

Since both sides of the previous equation are linear in
\alpha
, it follows that there is a unique vector bundle map
J:\ker\mathrm{d}\mu\to D
such that

J(\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)):=\widetilde{g^{\sharp}(\alpha)}.

(6.5)

Since
TS=\ker\mathrm{d}\mu\oplus D
, we obtain a vector bundle map
J:TS\to TS
by requiring
J^{2}=-I
. Notice that
J
is an invariant almost complex structure

\mathscr{L}_{\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)}J=0,\quad\text{ if }\quad\nabla\alpha=0,

and claim that

G(X,Y)=\omega(X,JY),\quad(X,Y\in TS).

To see this, we check that the bilinear form
\omega(\cdot,J\cdot)
satisfy properties (a)-(c) above:

(a)

Since
J(\ker\mathrm{d}\mu)=D
and
J(D)=\ker\mathrm{d}\mu
, and both distributions are Lagrangian, it follows that they are orthogonal relative to the bilinear form
\omega(\cdot,J\cdot)
.

(b)

By (
6.5
), it follows that

\displaystyle\omega(\widetilde{g^{\sharp}(\alpha)},J\widetilde{g^{\sharp}(\beta)})

\displaystyle=-\omega(\widetilde{g^{\sharp}(\alpha)},\mathbscr{a}(\beta))

\displaystyle=(\mu^{*}\beta)(\widetilde{g^{\sharp}(\alpha)})

\displaystyle=\beta(g^{\sharp}(\alpha))=h(g^{\sharp}(\alpha),g^{\sharp}(\beta)).

Hence, the restriction of
\mathrm{d}\mu
to
D
maps the bilinear form
\omega(\cdot,J\cdot)
to
g
.

(c)

Again by (
6.5
), it follows that

\displaystyle\omega(\mathbscr{a}(\alpha),J(\mathbscr{a}(\beta)))

\displaystyle=\omega(\mathbscr{a}(\alpha),\widetilde{g^{\sharp}(\beta)}))

\displaystyle=(\mu^{*}\alpha)(\widetilde{g^{\sharp}(\beta)})

\displaystyle=\alpha(g^{\sharp}(\beta))=g^{-1}(\alpha,\beta).

Hence, the last item is also satisfied.

It remains to show that
J
is integrable, i.e., that its Nijenhuis torsion vanishes. For this, note that:

[\mathbscr{a}(\alpha),J(\mathbscr{a}(\beta))]=(\mathscr{L}_{\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)}J)(\mathbscr{a}(\beta))+J([\mathbscr{a}(\alpha),\mathbscr{a}(\beta)])=0,\quad\text{ if }\quad\nabla\alpha=\nabla\beta=0,

(6.6)

since
J
is invariant and
\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)
and
\mathbscr{a}(\beta)
commute. On the other hand, since
g
is Hessian, we have for flat
\alpha
and
\beta
:

\displaystyle g^{\flat}([g^{\sharp}(\alpha),g^{\sharp}(\beta)])

\displaystyle=g^{\flat}([g^{\sharp}(\alpha),g^{\sharp}(\beta)])-\nabla_{g^{\sharp}(\alpha)}\beta+\nabla_{g^{\sharp}(\beta)}\alpha

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}g^{\flat}(g^{\sharp}(\alpha),g^{\sharp}(\beta))=0.

It follows that
[g^{\sharp}(\alpha),g^{\sharp}(\beta)]=0
, and using (
6.5
), we conclude that:

[J(\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)),J(\mathbscr{a}(\beta))]=0,\quad\text{ if }\quad\nabla\alpha=\nabla\beta=0.

(6.7)

From (
6.6
) and (
6.7
), we find that the Nijenhuis torsion of
J
satisfies:

N_{J}(\mathbscr{a}(\alpha),\mathbscr{a}(\beta))=N_{J}(\mathbscr{a}(\alpha),J(\mathbscr{a}(\beta)))=N_{J}(J(\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)),J(\mathbscr{a}(\beta)))=0,

for any flat sections
\alpha
and
\beta
. Since the vector fields
\mathbscr{a}(\alpha)
and
J(\mathbscr{a}(\alpha))
locally generate all vector fields, we conclude that
N_{J}\equiv 0
. This concludes the proof.
∎

6.2.
Abreu’s equation

The scalar curvature of an invariant Kähler structure on a Lagrangian fibration is an invariant smooth function on the total space, and hence corresponds to a smooth function on the base of the fibration. In his work on classical symplectic toric manifolds
[
1
]
, Abreu gave an explicit expression for this function in terms of integral affine coordinates and observed that the Kähler metric is extremal in the sense of Calabi (see, e.g.,
[
7
]
) if and only if this function is affine. This generalizes to toric
\mathcal{T}
-spaces. In this section we consider the case of Lagrangian fibrations.

Recall that for an affine manifold
(M,\nabla)
with a Hessian metric:

•

the
first Koszul form

\alpha
is the
1
-form on
M
defined by:

\alpha(X)\nu=\nabla_{X}\nu,\quad X\in\mathfrak{X}(M),

with
\nu
the volume density of the Hessian metric,

•

the
second Koszul form

\beta:=\nabla\alpha
is the symmetric
(0,2)
-tensor given by:

(\nabla\alpha)(X,Y)=(\nabla_{X}\alpha)(Y).

As will be clear from the proof below, the second Koszul form is an incarnation of the Ricci tensor in Hessian geometry. Further, recall that any integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
carries a canonical volume density
\nu_{\Lambda}
, which in any integral affine chart
(U,x^{i})
is given by

\nu_{\Lambda}|_{U}=|\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{n}|.

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
\mu:(S,\omega,G)\to M
be a Kähler Lagrangian fibration, inducing an integral affine structure
\Lambda
on
M
and a Hessian metric
g
on
(M,\Lambda)
. Let
\alpha
and
\beta
be the first and second Koszul forms of
(M,\Lambda,g)
,
\nu_{\Lambda}
the volume density of
\Lambda
and
\nabla^{g}
the Levi-Civita connection of
g
. The scalar curvature of
G
is given by

S_{G}=\mathrm{Tr}_{g}(2\nabla^{g}\alpha-\beta)\circ\mu=\mathrm{div}_{\nu_{\Lambda}}(g^{\sharp}(\alpha))\circ\mu.

(6.8)

Moreover,
G
is an extremal metric if and only if
S_{G}
is affine, viewed as function on the integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
.

Remark 6.7
.

(i)

Given a compact complex manifold
(S,J)
and a Kähler class, the Calabi functional is defined on the space of
J
-compatible Kähler metrics
G
in the given Kähler class and is given by

G\mapsto\int_{M}S_{G}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}v_{G},

(6.9)

where
v_{G}
is the Riemannian volume form.
A Kähler metric
G
is called
extremal
if it is a critical point of this functional
[
7
]
. Using the corresponding Euler-Lagrange equations one finds (see, e.g.,
[
34
]
) that a Kähler metric
G
is extremal if and only if

(\operatorname{grad}S_{G})^{(1,0)}\textrm{ is a holomorphic vector field.}

(E)

We adopt condition (
E
) as the definition of extremal Kähler metric, whether
S
is a compact manifold or not.

(ii)

In our approach, where we fix a symplectic manifold
(S,\omega)
, one has the following equivalent approach. The functional (
6.9
) is now defined on the space of
\omega
-compatible Kähler metrics
G
whose complex structure belong to a fixed symplectomorphism class. The critical points are still the Kähler metrics satisfying (
E
), which in symplectic terms can be reformulated as

X_{S_{G}}\textrm{ is a Killing vector field.}

(E’)

(iii)

In an integral affine chart
(U,x^{1},\dots,x^{n})
for
(M,\Lambda)
, the first Koszul form is given by

\alpha|_{U}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial\log(\det(g))}{\partial x^{j}}

and (
6.8
) yields

S_{G}|_{U}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{2}g^{jk}}{\partial x^{j}\partial x^{k}},

where
g^{-1}=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}g^{jk}\partial_{x^{j}}\partial_{x^{k}}
. The condition that the function
S_{G}
is affine then becomes a 4th order PDE for the potential of the metric
g
, known as Abreu’s equation.

Proof.

The proof is essentially as in
[
1
]
. In view of Corollary
6.5
and Remark
3.21
,
M
can be covered by opens
U
over which the given Kähler Lagrangian fibration is equivalent to the Lagrangian fibration
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})\to M
equipped with the Kähler structure induced as in Theorem
6.2
by
g
and the canonical horizontal distribution. So, we can assume that
(S,\omega)=(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
and
\mu
is the bundle projection.

Given an integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
with a Hessian metric
g
, there is another integral affine structure on
M
defined by the lattice dual to

\Lambda^{\sharp}:=g^{\sharp}(\Lambda)\subset TM.

The metric
g
is Hessian with respect to
\Lambda^{\sharp}
as well. This defines the dual Hessian structure, in the terminology of
[
33
]
. The second Koszul form of
(M,\Lambda^{\sharp},g)
is (see
[
33
, Lemma 3.1]
)

\beta_{\sharp}=\beta-2\nabla^{g}\alpha.

(6.10)

Like
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}=T^{*}M/\Lambda
, the torus bundle
\pi:TM/{\Lambda^{\sharp}}\to M
comes with a canonical horizontal distribution and vector bundle isomorphism
\mathbscr{a}:\pi^{*}TM\xrightarrow{\sim}\ker(\mathrm{d}\pi)
. Moreover,
TM/{\Lambda^{\sharp}}
comes with a natural complex structure
J
given by

J(\mathbscr{a}(X))=\widetilde{X},\quad J(\widetilde{X})=-\mathbscr{a}(X),

and the metric
g
induces a metric
G_{\sharp}
on
TM/{\Lambda^{\sharp}}
given by

G_{\sharp}(\mathbscr{a}(X_{1})+\widetilde{Y_{1}},\mathbscr{a}(X_{2})+\widetilde{Y_{2}})=g(X_{1},X_{2})+g(Y_{1},Y_{2}),

where
\widetilde{X}
denotes the horizontal lift of
X\in TM
. These define a Kähler structure, which corresponds to the above Kähler structure on
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
via the torus bundle isomorphism:

TM/{\Lambda^{\sharp}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}

(6.11)

induced by
g
(see, e.g.,
[
5
]
). It follows from
[
33
, Proposition 3.5]
that the Ricci tensor
R_{\sharp}
of
G_{\sharp}
is given by:

R_{\sharp}(\mathbscr{a}(X_{1})+\widetilde{Y_{1}},\mathbscr{a}(X_{2})+\widetilde{Y_{2}})=-\left(\beta_{\sharp}(X_{1},X_{2})+\beta_{\sharp}(Y_{1},Y_{2})\right),

with
\beta_{\sharp}
the second Koszul form of
(M,\Lambda^{\sharp},g)
. From this it readily follows that the scalar curvature
S_{G}
(which equals that of
G_{\sharp}
, since (
6.11
) is an isometry) is equal to
-\textrm{Tr}_{g}(\beta_{\sharp})\circ\mu
, which proves the first equality in (
6.8
) in view of (
6.10
). The second equality follows from Remark
6.7
.

A straightforward computation using action-angle coordinates and expression (
6.4
) for
G
shows that for a function
f
, depending only on the action coordinates, the Hamiltonian vector field
X_{f}
is a Killing vector field for
G
if and only if
f
is affine. This yields the equivalence of the extremal condition (
E’
) with the condition for
S_{G}
.
∎

6.3.
Examples

We will now illustrate how one can use Theorems
6.2
and
6.6
to construct extremal Kähler metrics on Lagrangian fibrations.

Example 6.8
.

Any metric on
(\mathbb{S}^{1},b\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{d}x)
, with
b>0
, is Hessian (for dimensional reasons) and of the form
f\mathrm{d}x^{2}
, for
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
strictly positive. It follows from (
6.8
) that the scalar curvature of any invariant Kähler metric
G
on the complex torus that induces this Hessian metric is given by

S_{G}=\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{2f^{2}}-\frac{(f^{\prime})^{2}}{f^{3}}.

(6.12)

Since
\mathbb{S}^{1}
is compact, if
S_{G}
is affine, then it is in fact constant. Since
f
is strictly positive and periodic, it follows that this constant must then be zero. The only solutions to the ODE

ff^{\prime\prime}-2(f^{\prime})^{2}=0,

are the constant functions on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Therefore, we conclude that the only extremal metrics are those for which
f
is constant.

As we saw in Example
3.23
, any Lagrangian fibration inducing
(M,\Lambda)
takes the form

\operatorname{pr}_{2}:(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{1},b\,\mathrm{d}y\wedge\mathrm{d}x)\to\mathbb{S}^{1}.

If we fix the Hessian metric
g=b^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x^{2}
and choose any of the Lagrangian connections
D_{a}
in that example, the resulting extremal Kähler metric on
S=\mathbb{T}^{2}
is the flat metric

G=(a^{2}+b^{2})\,\mathrm{d}x^{2}+2a\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y+\mathrm{d}y^{2}.

The
2
-torus equipped with corresponding complex structure is biholomorphic to the complex torus
\mathbb{C}/\langle 1,\tau\rangle
, where
\tau=a+ib
.

Example 6.9
.

Consider the Lagrangian fibration
\operatorname{pr}_{1}:(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{2},\omega_{a})\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
of Examples
3.7
and
3.24
, where
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
\omega_{a}=\omega_{\mathrm{can}}+a\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}
. This admits the flat Lagrangian connection

D=\langle\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}}+a\partial_{\theta_{1}}\rangle.

If we consider the flat Hessian metric
g=\mathrm{d}x^{1}\mathrm{d}x^{1}+\mathrm{d}x^{2}\mathrm{d}x^{2}
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
, we obtain from Theorem
6.2
the
\omega_{a}
-compatible extremal Kähler metric

G=\mathrm{d}x^{1}\mathrm{d}x^{1}+(1+a^{2})\mathrm{d}x^{2}\mathrm{d}x^{2}-2a\mathrm{d}x^{1}\mathrm{d}\theta_{2}+\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}+\mathrm{d}\theta_{2}\mathrm{d}\theta_{2}.

Notice that, when one varies
a\in[0,1[
, these give non-isomorphic Kähler metrics.

Example 6.10
.

Consider the standard integral affine cylinder

(M,\Lambda)=(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{d}x\oplus\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{d}h)

of Example
2.20
. Given a metric

g=A\mathrm{d}h^{2}+2B\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}x+C\mathrm{d}x^{2}

on this cylinder, We find that

\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}g^{\flat}(\partial_{h},\partial_{x})=\left(\partial_{h}B-\partial_{x}A\right)\mathrm{d}h+\left(\partial_{h}C-\partial_{x}B\right)\mathrm{d}x.

Therefore, we have a family of Hessian metrics

g=\frac{1}{\tau(h)}\mathrm{d}h^{2}+\frac{1}{\epsilon(x)}\mathrm{d}x^{2},

where both
\tau(h)
and
\epsilon(x)
are strictly positive.

Note that
H^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})=H^{2}(M,\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda})=0
, so that any toric
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-space is isomorphic to the symplectic torus bundle over
(M,\Lambda)
(see Example
2.20
).
Applying (
6.8
), it follows that the scalar curvature of the lifted Kähler metric
G
on this symplectic torus bundle is given by

S_{G}=-\frac{1}{2}(\tau^{\prime\prime}(h)+\epsilon^{\prime\prime}(x)),

for any choice of flat Lagrangian connection. The condition for
G
to be extremal, i.e., for
S_{G}
to be affine, amounts to the condition that
\epsilon(x)
is constant and
\tau(h)
is a cubic polynomial in
h
. Since
\tau(h)
is strictly positive, this leads to the following two possibilities for
G
to be extremal:

(i)

\tau(h)=bh^{2}+ch+d
with
b>0
and
c^{2}-4bd<0
, in which case
G
has constant negative scalar curvature
-\tfrac{b}{2}
;

(ii)

\tau(h)
is constant, in which case
G
has zero scalar curvature.

Example 6.11
.

Consider the non-standard integral affine cylinder of Example
2.21
. Given a general metric on
\mathbb{S}^{1}\times]-2,\infty[
, written in the form

g=A\,\mathrm{d}h^{2}+2B\,\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}x+C\,\mathrm{d}x^{2}.

We find that

\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}g^{\flat}(\partial_{h},\partial_{x})=\left(\partial_{h}B-\partial_{x}A+\tfrac{B}{h+2}\right)\mathrm{d}h+\left(\partial_{h}C-\partial_{x}B-\tfrac{C}{h+2}\right)\mathrm{d}x.

Therefore, we have a family of Hessian metrics

g=\frac{1}{\tau(h)}\mathrm{d}h^{2}+(h+2)\epsilon\,\mathrm{d}x^{2},

where
\epsilon>0
and
\tau(h)
is a strictly positive smooth function on
]-2,\infty[
.

Like in the previous example,
H^{1}(M,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Lag}}})=H^{2}(M,\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda})=0
, so any toric
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-space is isomorphic to the symplectic torus bundle over
(M,\Lambda)
(see Example
2.21
). Applying (
6.8
), it follows that the scalar curvature of the lifted Kähler metric
G
on this symplectic torus bundle is given by

S_{G}=-\frac{1}{2}\tau^{\prime\prime}(h)-\frac{1}{h+2}\tau^{\prime}(h),

for any choice of flat Lagrangian connection. Such a metric is extremal if

\frac{1}{2}\tau^{\prime\prime}(h)+\frac{1}{h+2}\tau^{\prime}(h)=c_{1}(h+2)+c_{0}

for some constants
c_{1}
and
c_{0}
. One finds that

\tau(h)=\frac{c_{1}}{6}(h+2)^{3}+\frac{c_{0}}{3}(h+2)^{2}+\frac{c_{2}}{h+2}+c_{3}

where
c_{2}
and
c_{3}
are constants of integration. Therefore, choosing the constants such that
\tau(h)>0
, we obtain extremal Kähler metrics on the symplectic torus bundle. These include examples both with constant and non-constant scalar curvature.

7.
Invariant Kähler metrics II: toric Lagrangian fibrations

In this section we will generalize the characterization of compatible invariant Kähler metrics on Lagrangian fibrations given in Theorem
6.2
to toric
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-spaces.

Below, in Section
7.1
, we will introduce the notion of
hybrid
b
-metric
for manifolds with corners. These are metrics admitting a specific type of singularity at the boundary and with a well-defined notion of residue at the open facets. Using this notion, we can state the main result of this section as follows.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
(M,\Lambda)
be an integral affine manifold and let
\mu:(S,\omega)\to M
be a toric
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
-space, with Delzant subspace
\Delta:=\mu(S)
. Denote by
\mathring{\Delta}
the interior of
\Delta
and set
\mathring{S}:=\mu^{-1}(\mathring{\Delta})
. For any invariant Kähler metric
G
on
S
compatible with
\omega
, the following hold:

(i)

(\ker\mathrm{d}\mu)^{\perp}|_{\mathring{S}}
extends to an elliptic connection with zero radial residue over the open facets;

(ii)

The Hessian metric
g
on
\mathring{\Delta}
, induced via Theorem
6.2
, extends to a hybrid
b
-metric on
\Delta
with the residue at the open facets given by the primitive outward-pointing normals multiplied by
\tfrac{1}{4\pi}
.

Conversely, given a flat Lagrangian elliptic connection
\theta
with zero radial residues and a hybrid
b
-metric
g
on
\Delta
with residues as in (ii), there is a unique invariant compatible Kähler metric
G
on
S
inducing
g
and such that
\ker\theta|_{\mathring{S}}=(\ker\mathrm{d}\mu)^{\perp}|_{\mathring{S}}
.

Remark 7.2
.

The scalar curvature
S_{G}
of an invariant Kähler metric
G
descends to a smooth function on
\Delta
, because it is
\mathcal{T}
-invariant. It follows from the case of smooth Lagrangian fibrations (by restricting to
\mathring{\Delta}
) that
G
is an extremal Kähler metric if and only if
S_{G}:\Delta\to\mathbb{R}
is an affine function.

By the same reasoning as for Corollary
6.5
, we conclude the following from Theorems
4.33
and
7.1
.

Corollary 7.3
.

Given an integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
and a Delzant subspace
\Delta
, there is a canonical 1:1 correspondence

\left\{\\
\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-12.0pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces K\"{a}hler toric $\mathcal{T}$-spaces}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces$\mu:(S,\omega,G)\to M$ with $\mu(S)=\Delta$}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces up to equivalence \,}\crcr}}}}\right\}\ \tilde{\longleftrightarrow}\ \left\{\\
\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-6.94444pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces Hessian hybrid }\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces\,$b$-metrics $g$ on $\Delta$\,}\crcr}}}}\right\}\ \times\check{H}^{1}(\Delta,\underline{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{Flat}}})}}}}}

7.1.
Hybrid
b
-metrics

Let
\Delta
be a manifold with corners. Given a smooth section
